Faster Fixed Parameter Tractable Algorithms for Counting Markov Equivalence Classes with Special Skeletons
Vidya Sagar Sharma

Abstract
The structure of Markov equivalence classes (MECs) of causal DAGs has been studied extensively. A natural question in this regard is to algorithmically find the number of MECs with a given skeleton. Until recently, the known results for this problem were in the setting of very special graphs (such as paths, cycles and star graphs). More recently, a fixed-parameter tractable (FPT) algorithm was given for this problem which, given an input graph G G , counts the number of MECs with the skeleton G G in O ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( 2 O ⁡ ( d 4 ​ k 4 ) + n 2 ) ) O(n(2^{O(d^{4}k^{4})}+n^{2})) time, where n n , d d , and k k , respectively, are the numbers of nodes, the treewidth, and the degree of G G . We give a faster FPT algorithm that solves the problem in O ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( 2 O ⁡ ( d 2 ​ k 2 ) + n 2 ) ) O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2})) time when the input graph is chordal. Additionally, we show that the runtime can be further improved to polynomial time when the input graph G G is a tree.



1
Introduction

A graphical model is a graphical way to represent the conditional dependence between the random variables. Both the directed and the undirected versions of the graphical models are well-studied in the literature. The directed graphical models are of two kinds: cyclic and acyclic. We study the directed acyclic graphical model, also known as the Bayesian network.

In the Bayesian network, a directed acyclic graph (DAG) with vertex set
V
represents a probability distribution on the set of random variables
V
if for each node
w
in the DAG, the probability distribution satisfies the conditional independence relation that
w
is independent of its non-descendants given its parent. A DAG entails a conditional independence relation if all the probability distribution that the DAG represents satisfies the conditional independence relation. There can be more than one DAG that entails the same set of conditional independence relations. Such DAGs are said to be Markov equivalent.
Verma and Pearl 1990
show the graphical resemblance between two Markov equivalent DAGs. They show that two DAGs are Markov equivalent if, and only if, both have the same skeleton (underlying undirected graph), and both have the same set of v-structures (induced subgraphs of the form
a\rightarrow b\leftarrow c
). Markov equivalence classes (MECs) are used to partition the DAGs. Two Markov equivalent DAGs belong to the same MEC. In other words, an MEC is a maximal size set of Markov equivalent DAGs.

An MEC entails the same set of conditional independence relations between the random variables that are entailed by the DAGs it contains. An MEC is graphically represented by the union of DAGs it contains. We treat an MEC and its graphical representation as the same.
Andersson et al. 1997
give necessary and sufficient conditions for a graph to be an MEC (
Theorem

2.2
).

Meek 1995
gives a method to construct an MEC given the set of conditional independence relations entailed by the MEC. He shows that two random variables
A
and
B
are not adjacent in the MEC if, and only if, the MEC entails a conditional independence relation of the form
A\perp B\mid S
for some
S\subseteq V\setminus{\{A,B\}}
, where
V
is the set of nodes of the MEC (
(
Meek 1995
, p. 3)
).
Suppose
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are two MECs with the same skeleton and entail the set of conditional independence relations, respectively,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, on the set of random variables
V
.

Meek 1995
’s result shows that for two random variables
A
and
B
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
contains
A\perp B\mid S_{1}
for some
S_{1}\subseteq V\setminus{\{A,B\}}
if, and only if,
\mathcal{M}_{2}
contains
A\perp B\mid S_{2}
for some
S_{2}\subseteq V\setminus{\{A,B\}}
(
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
may not be the same).

This shows that the two MECs with the same skeleton have not only a graphical relation but also a statistical one. This connection between MECs with the same skeleton gives us the motivation to solve the problem of counting MECs having the same skeleton. In particular, a natural question to ask is: given an undirected graph
G
, how many MECs have the same skeleton as
G
?

Related Work

Counting MECs is a well-studied problem.
Perlman 2001
written a computer program that for input
n
, outputs the number of MECs with
n
nodes.
Gillispie and Perlman 2002
written a computer program that for input
n
, enumerates the MECs. They also studied the distribution of the size of MECs. They observed that the ratio of DAGs to the number of MECs asymptotically converges to 3.7.
Steinsky 2003
gives a recursive formula that counts the number of MECs of size 1.
Gillispie 2006
come up with a recursive formula that counts MECs of any size.
Schmid and Sly 2022
show that as
n
tends to infinity the ratio of the number of DAGs with
n
nodes and the number of MECs with
n
nodes approaches a positive constant.

The above results were on the classes of MECs with a given number of nodes. We now focus on the results of the classes of MECs with a fixed skeleton.
Verma and Pearl 1990
’s result shows that each MEC has a unique skeleton and a set of v-structures.
Radhakrishnan et al. 2016
work on counting MECs with the same skeleton. They partitioned the set of MECs based on the number of v-structures in them and constructed a generating function for counting MECs. They show experimentally that even for the graphs with the same number of nodes, the generating function varies. Their further work on the problem of counting MECs with the same skeleton explored generating functions for specific graph structures such as path graphs, cycle graphs, star graphs, and bi-star graphs (
Radhakrishnan et al. 2018
). Their result also provides a tight lower and upper bound for the number of MECs with a tree skeleton. Recently,
Sharma 2023
give a fixed parameter tractable algorithm that for an input graph
G
, counts the number of MECs with skeleton
G
in time
O(n(2^{O(k^{4}d^{4})}+n^{2}))
, where
n
,
k
and
d
are, respectively, the number nodes of
G
, the treewidth of
G
, and the degree of
G
.

Our Contribution and Organization of the Paper

We provide a faster-fixed parameter tractable algorithm (
Algorithm

3
) that for a chordal graph
G
, counts MECs with skeleton
G
in time
O(n(2^{O(k^{2}d^{2})}+n^{2}))
, where
n
,
k
and
d
are, respectively, the number nodes of
G
, the treewidth of
G
, and the degree of
G
. We also provide a polynomial algorithm (
Algorithm

1
) that for a tree graph
G
, counts MECs with skeleton
G
in time
O(n^{2}d)
, where
n
and
d
are, respectively, the number of nodes of
G
, and the degree of
G
. Our result is a significant improvement over the present best known solution given by
Sharma 2023
for the chordal and tree graphs. The current best result for counting MECs with a tree skeleton is by
Sharma 2023
with the time complexity exponential in the degree of the tree graph, whereas our algorithm for counting MECs with a tree skeleton is polynomial in the size of the input graph.

We define the terminologies used in the paper in
section

2
. In
section

3.2.1
, we formally define the problem of counting MECs with the same skeleton (
1
). In
section

3.1
, we solve
1
when the skeleton is a tree graph. In
section

3.2
, we solve
1
when the skeleton is a chordal graph.
section

4
deals with the time complexity of the algorithms studied in the paper. All the missing proofs of the main section are provided in the Supplementary Material.

2
Preliminary

Graph Terminologies

We follow the graph theory terminologies used by
Andersson et al. 1997
and
Sharma 2023
. A graph
G=(V,E)
is a tuple, where
V
is the set of nodes, and
E\subseteq V\times V
is the set of edges of
G
. For a graph
G
, we denote the set of nodes of
G
by
V_{G}
and the set of edges of
G
by
E_{G}
. An edge
(u,v)
is said to be an
undirected edge
of
G
, denoted as
u-v\in E_{G}
, if both
(u,v)
and
(v,u)
are in
E_{G}
. An edge
(u,v)
is said to be a
directed edge
of
G
, denoted as
u\rightarrow v\in E_{G}
, if
(u,v)\in E_{G}
and
(v,u)\notin E_{G}
. A graph is said to be an
undirected graph
if each edge of the graph is undirected. A graph is said to be a
directed graph
if each edge of the graph is directed. A sequence of distinct nodes
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{l})
is said to be a
path
of a graph
G
if for each
1\leq i<l
,
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in E_{G}
.

P
is said to be a path from
(u,v)
to
(x,y)
if the first two nodes of
P
are
u
and
v
and the last two nodes are
x
and
y
. Similarly,
P
is said to be a path from
(u,v)
to
w
if the first two nodes of the path are
u
and
v
and the last node of the path is
w
.
A sequence
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{l},u_{l+1}=u_{1})
is said to be a
cycle
if
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{l}
are distinct and for each
1\leq i\leq l
,
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in E_{G}
. A cycle of a graph is said to be a
directed cycle
of the graph if one edge of the cycle is a directed edge in the graph. A cycle
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{l},u_{l+1}=u_{1})
of a graph is said to be a
chordless cycle
of the graph if there does not exist any edge between any non-adjacent nodes of the cycle. A graph that does not have any directed cycle is said to be a
chain graph
. A graph is said to be a
chordal graph
if each cycle of the graph is chordless. A
skeleton
of a graph is the underlying undirected graph of it, i.e., we get the skeleton of a graph by replacing each directed edge
u\rightarrow v
of the graph with an undirected edge
u-v
.

\textup{skeleton}({G})
denotes the the skeleton of
G
.
A
v-structure
of a graph is an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b\leftarrow c
. For a graph
G
,
\mathcal{V}(G)
is denoted as the set of v-structures of
G
.
N(X,G)
denotes the set of nodes of
G
that are a neighbor of a node in
X
. A clique of an undirected graph
G
is a set of nodes
C
of
G
such that for each pair of nodes
u,v\in C
,
(u,v)\in E_{G}
.

Definition 2.1

(
Union of graphs
)
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two graphs.
G
is said to be the
union
of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
if
V_{G}=V_{G_{1}}\cup V_{G_{2}}
and
E_{G}=E_{G_{1}}\cup E_{G_{2}}
.

Markov Equivalence Class (MEC)

We have defined MEC in the introduction.
Andersson et al. 1997
give the following necessary and sufficient conditions for a graph to be an MEC (
Theorem

2.2
).

(a):

u
v
w

(b):

u
v
w

(c):

u
v
w

(d):

u
v
w^{\prime}
w

Figure 1:
Strongly protected
u\rightarrow v
.

Theorem 2.2

(
Andersson et al. 1997
)
.

A graph
G
is an MEC if, and only if,

1.

G
is a chain graph.

2.

For every chain component
\tau
of
G
,
G_{\tau}
is chordal, i.e., every undirected connected component of
G
is chordal.

3.

The configuration
a\rightarrow b-c
does not occur as an induced subgraph of
G
.

4.

Every directed edge
u\rightarrow v\in G
is strongly protected in
G
, i.e.,
u\rightarrow v
is a part of at least one of the induced subgraphs of
G
of the form as shown in
Figure

1
.

For an MEC
M
with skeleton
G
, we say
M
is an MEC of
G
. We denote the set of MECs of
G
with
\text{MEC}(G)
.

The following definitions and results come from
Sharma 2023
.

Definition 2.3

(
Partial MEC
,
Sharma 2023
)
.

A graph
G
is said to be a partial MEC if it obeys
items

1
,
2
and
3
of
theorem

2.2
.
A partial MEC
O
is said to be a partial MEC of
G
, if
\textup{skeleton}({O})=G
.

\text{PMEC}(G)
denotes the set of partial MECs with skeleton
G
.

Observation 2.4

(
Sharma 2023
)
.

For an MEC
G
, its induced subgraphs are partial MECs.

Definition 2.5

(
Projection
,
Sharma 2023
)
.

Suppose
G
is an undirected graph,
G^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph of
G
, and
M
and
M^{\prime}
are MECs of, respectively,
G
and
G^{\prime}
. We say
M^{\prime}
is a projection of
M
on
Y=V_{G^{\prime}}
if the set of v-structures of
M^{\prime}
and the set of v-structures of
M[Y]
are the same, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(M[Y])
. We denote the projection of
M
on
Y
with
\mathcal{P}(M,Y)
. With a slight abuse of notation, we define
\mathcal{P}(M,Y_{1},Y_{2})=(\mathcal{P}(M,Y_{1}),\mathcal{P}(M,Y_{2}))
.

Observation 2.6

(
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
M
be an MEC of
G
. For all
Y\subseteq V_{G}
, there exists a unique projection of
M
on
Y
, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}(M,Y)
is unique.

Lemma 2.7

(
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
M
be a MEC, and
M^{\prime}
is a projection of
M
. If
u\rightarrow v\in M^{\prime}
then
u\rightarrow v\in M
.

Definition 2.8

(
Clique Tree Representation
,
Blair and Peyton 1993
)
.

For a chordal graph, a clique tree representation of a chordal graph
G
is a tree graph
T
such that (a)
V_{T}=\Pi(G)
, a set of maximal cliques of
G
,
and (b)
T
obeys the
clique intersection property
: for
x,y\in V_{T}
, if
z
is a node along the path between
x
and
y
in
T
then
x\cap y\subseteq z
.

Lemma 2.9

(
Blair and Peyton 1993
)
.

For every chordal graph
G
, a clique tree representation of
G
exists.

Definition 2.10

(
LBFS odering
,
Rose et al. 1976
)
.

An LBFS ordering
\tau
of a chordal graph
G
is an ordering of the nodes of
G
with the property that for any node
u
of the graph, the neighbors of
u
that comes before
u
in
\tau
form a clique, i.e., if
x-u,y-u\in E_{G}
,
\tau(x)<\tau(u)
, and
\tau(y)<\tau(u)
then
x-y\in E_{G}
.

Lemma 2.11

(
Rose et al. 1976
)
.

For a chordal
G
, for any clique
C
, there exists an LBFS ordering that starts with
C
.

Definition 2.12

(
Synchronous Graphs
,
Sharma 2023
)
.

Two graphs
G
and
H
are said to be
synchronous graphs
if there do not exist vertices
x,y\in V_{G}\cap V_{H}
such that
x\rightarrow y\in E_{G}
and
y\rightarrow x\in E_{H}
.

Definition 2.13

(
Markov Union of Synchronous Graphs
,
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{l}
be pairwise synchronous graphs. The
Markov union
of
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{l}
is a graph
G
, denoted by
G=U_{M}(G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{l})
, such that
\textup{skeleton}({G})
is the skeleton of the union of
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{l}
(see
definition

2.1
for the definition of the graph union), and for any edge
u-v\in\textup{skeleton}({G})
,
u\rightarrow v\in G
if
u\rightarrow v\in G_{i}
for any
G_{i}
. More formally,
V_{G}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}V_{G_{i}}
, the set of directed edges of
G
is
D_{G}=\{u\rightarrow v:u\rightarrow v\in E_{G_{i}}
for some
1\leq i\leq l\}
, and the set of undirected edges of
G
is
U_{G}=\{u-v:u-v\in E_{G_{i}}
for some
1\leq i\leq l
, and there does not exist a
j
such that
u\rightarrow v\in E_{G_{j}}
or
v\rightarrow u\in E_{G_{j}}\}
. In other words, if
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in any graph
G_{i}
, then
u\rightarrow v
is also a directed edge in the Markov union graph, and an undirected edge
u-v
of
G_{i}
becomes an undirected edge of
G
only if neither
u\rightarrow v
nor
v\rightarrow u
is part of any graph in the union. Since the graphs are pairwise synchronous, if one graph
G_{i}
contains a directed edge
u\rightarrow v
, then no other graph
G_{j}
can contain the directed edge
v\rightarrow u
.

All proofs omitted from the main text are provided in the Supplementary Material.

3
Counting MECs with a tree or a chordal graph skeleton

We start with a formal definition of the problem.

Problem 1

(Counting MECs of an undirected graph)
.

Input:
An undirected graph
G
.

Output:
The number of MECs of
G
, i.e.,
|\text{MEC}(G)|
.

We solve the problem when
G
is a tree graph or a chordal graph.

3.1
Counting MECs of a tree graph

In this subsection, we solve
1
when
G
is a tree graph. We provide an algorithm that for a tree graph
G
, counts the MECs of
G
.
We start with the tree version of
theorem

2.2
.

Theorem 3.1
.

A graph
G
with a tree skeleton is an MEC if, and only if,

1.

G
does not have an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
.

2.

For each directed edge
u\rightarrow v
, there exists a node
w
such that
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
G
of the form either (a)
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
, or (b)
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
.

Proof.

A graph with a tree skeleton cannot have a cycle. Therefore, it neither has any directed cycle nor any chordless cycle. Consequently, the graph must be a chain graph. Additionally, each undirected connected component of the graph must be chordal. This implies that for a graph with a tree skeleton,
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

2.2
is always true.

Also, in a graph with a tree skeleton, there cannot be any induced subgraph of the form as shown in
fig.

1
.c and
fig.

1
.d. Therefore, if an edge of the graph is strongly protected then it must be due to part of an induced subgraph of the form either
fig.

1
.a, or
fig.

1
.b.
∎

To count MECs of a tree graph
G
, we arbitrarily pick a node
r_{1}
of
G
as its root node. We initially partition the set of MECs of
G
into two parts: (a) the set of MECs that have an incoming edge adjacent to
r_{1}
, denoted as
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and (b) the set of MECs that have no incoming edge adjacent to
r_{1}
, denoted as
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0)
. For simplicity, for any tree graph
H
with root node
r
, for
i\in\{0,1\}
, we denote
|\text{MEC}(H,r,i)|
by
n_{i}(H,r)
.
This implies

|\text{MEC}(G)|=n_{0}(G,r_{1})+n_{1}(G,r_{1})

(1)

Let the degree of
r_{1}
in
G
is
\delta
. We partition
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0)
into
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,0)
,
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,1)
, …,
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,\delta)
, where
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
is the set of MECs of
G
for which out of
\delta
edges adjacent to
r_{1}
,
i
edges are undirected and the remaining edges are outgoing edges.
Figure

2
is given for better understanding. For simplicity, for any tree graph
H
with root node
r
, we denote
|\text{MEC}(H,r,0,i)|
by
n_{0}^{i}(H,r)
. This implies

n_{0}(G,r_{1})=\sum_{i=0}^{\delta}{n_{0}^{i}(G,r_{1})}

(2)

r
a
b
c
d
G
r
a
b
c
d
MEC
M_{1}
r
a
b

MEC

Figure 2:

G
is a tree graph with root node
r
.
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are MECs (both obey
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
) with skeleton
G
.
M_{1}
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r,1)
as there is an edge
a\rightarrow r
incoming towards
r
.
M_{2}
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r,0)
as none of the edges adjacent to
r
in
M_{2}
is incoming towards
r
.
M_{2}
also belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r,0,1)
as it belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r,0)
and one edge adjacent to
r
in
M_{2}
is undirected.

We give a recursive algorithm to compute
n_{0}^{i}(G,r_{1})
and
n_{1}(G,r_{1})
. Pick a neighbor
r_{2}
of
r_{1}
in
G
. Cut the edge
r_{1}-r_{2}
in
G
. This will give us two induced subgraphs of
G
:
G_{1}
(containing node
r_{1}
) and
G_{2}
(containing node
r_{2}
). The degree of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}
must be
\delta_{1}=\delta-1
, where
\delta
is the degree of
r_{1}
in
G
. Let the degree of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}
be
\delta_{2}
.
Lemma

3.2
shows that if we have the knowledge of
n_{1}(G_{1},r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{0}(G_{1},r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{1}(G_{1},r_{1})
, …,
n_{0}^{\delta_{1}}(G_{1},r_{1})
, and

n_{1}(G_{2},r_{2})
,
n_{0}^{0}(G_{2},r_{2})
,
n_{0}^{1}(G_{2},r_{2})
, …,
n_{0}^{\delta_{2}}(G_{2},r_{2})
then we can compute
n_{1}(G,r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{0}(G,r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{1}(G,r_{1})
, …,
n_{0}^{\delta}(G,r_{1})
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
G
be a tree graph, and
(r_{1},r_{2})\in E_{G}
be an edge of
G
. By cutting the edge
(r_{1},r_{2})
, we get two induced subgraphs of
G
:
G_{1}
(containing node
r_{1}
) and
G_{2}
(containing node
r_{2}
). Then,

1.

n_{1}(G,r_{1})=n_{1}(G_{1},r_{1})\times[2\cdot n_{1}(G_{2},r_{2})+\sum_{j}{(j+2)\cdot n_{0}^{j}(G_{2},r_{2})}]+\sum_{i}{n_{0}^{i}(G_{1},r_{1})\times[(i+1)\cdot n_{1}(G_{2},r_{2})+i\cdot{n_{0}(G_{2},r_{2})}]}

2.

n_{0}^{0}(G,r_{1})=n_{0}^{0}(G_{1},r_{1})\times[n_{1}(G_{2},r_{2})+\sum_{j}{j.n_{0}^{j}(G_{2},r_{2})}]

3.

For
1\leq i\leq\delta
,

n_{0}^{i}(G,r_{1})=n_{0}^{i}(G_{1},r_{1})\times[n_{1}(G_{2},r_{2})+\sum_{j}{j.n_{0}^{j}(G_{2},r_{2})}]+n_{0}^{i-1}(G_{1},r_{1})\times{n_{0}(G_{2},r_{2})}

Input :
A tree graph
G
, a node
r_{1}\in V_{G}

Output :

(n_{1}(G,r_{1}),n_{0}^{0}(G,r_{1}),n_{0}^{1}(G,r_{1}),\ldots,n_{0}^{\delta}(G,r_{1}))
, where
\delta
is the degree of
r_{1}
in
G
.

1

\delta\leftarrow
degree of
r_{1}
in
G

2

if

\delta
= 0

then

3

return (0,1) end if

4

5

Pick an edge
(r_{1},r_{2})\in E_{G}
.

6

Cut the edge
(r_{1},r_{2})
.

7

G_{1}\leftarrow
subtree of
G
containing
r_{1}
after cutting the edge
(r_{1},r_{2})
of
G
.

8

G_{2}\leftarrow
subtree of
G
containing
r_{2}
after cutting the edge
(r_{1},r_{2})
of
G
.

9

\delta_{1}\leftarrow
degree of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}

10

\delta_{2}\leftarrow
degree of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}

11

(b_{1},c_{1}^{0},c_{1}^{1},\ldots,c_{1}^{\delta_{1}})\leftarrow
Counting-MEC-Tree(
G_{1},r_{1})

12

(b_{2},c_{2}^{0},c_{2}^{1},\ldots,c_{2}^{\delta_{2}})\leftarrow
Counting-MEC-Tree(
G_{2},r_{2})

13

N\leftarrow c_{2}^{0}+c_{2}^{1}+\ldots+c_{2}^{\delta_{2}}

14

b\leftarrow b_{1}\times[2\cdot b_{2}+\sum_{j}{(j+2)\cdot c_{2}^{j}}]+\sum_{i}{c_{1}^{i}\times[(i+1)\cdot b_{2}+i\cdot N]}

15

c_{0}\leftarrow c_{1}^{0}\times[b_{2}+\sum_{j}{j\cdot c_{2}^{j}}]

16

i\leftarrow 1

17

while

i\leq\delta

do

18

c_{i}\leftarrow c_{1}^{i}\times[b_{2}+\sum_{j}{j\cdot c_{2}^{j}}]+c_{1}^{i-1}\times N

19

i=i+1

end while

20

21

return

(b,c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{\delta})

Algorithm 1

Counting-MEC-Tree(
G,r_{1}
)

Proof sketch of
item

1
of
lemma

3.2
.

From
2.6
, for each MEC
M
of
G
,
(M_{1},M_{2})=\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})
is unique. This implies that for each MEC
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, there exists a unique pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
(M_{1},M_{2})=\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})
. The pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
can fall into four categories: either (a)
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, or (b)
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
, or (c)
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, or (d)
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
. For each possible pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
, we determine the number of MECs
M
belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. By counting all such
M
, we obtain the required
n_{1}(G,r_{1})
.

In the case of each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})
, where
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, two MECs
M
exist, both of which belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, with their projections on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
being
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively. One possible
M
is the union of
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
, and the other possible
M
is the union of
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
. Verification of these
M
s regarding their status as an MEC is based on satisfying both
item

1
and
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Similarly, in the second scenario, for each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})
, where
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
j+2
MECs
M
exist belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
with projections on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
as
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively.

For the third case, for each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
,
i+1
MECs
M
belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
with projections of
M
on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
as
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively.

In the fourth scenario, for each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
i
MECs
M
belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
with projections on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
as
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively.

Counting all the aforementioned possible
M
gives us the required number of MECs belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, i.e.,
n_{1}(G,r_{1})
. This proves
item

1
of
lemma

3.2
.
∎

Proof of
items

2
and
3
of
lemma

3.2
follows the same approach as the proof of
item

1
of
lemma

3.2
. The complete proof of
lemma

3.2
is provided in the Supplementary Material.

We construct
algorithm

1
to count the MECs of a tree graph. Lines
1
–
1
handle the base case when the degree of the root node in the tree graph
G
is 0, i.e., when
G
has only one node. In this scenario, only one MEC of
G
is possible, which is
G
itself. This MEC belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r,0,0)
because in the MEC, no edge adjacent to
r
is an incoming edge towards
r
, and no edge adjacent to
r
is undirected. This implies that when
G
has only one node,
n_{1}(G,r)=0
, and
n_{0}^{0}(G,r)=1
. Line
1
reflects the same. The remaining lines correctly implement
lemma

3.2
.

We now proceed to the second part of the paper, where we construct an algorithm that counts the MECs of a chordal graph.

3.2
Counting MECs of a chordal graph

In this subsection, we address
1
when
G
is a chordal graph. We give an algorithm that for a chordal graph
G
, counts the MECs of
G
.
The idea of this algorithm comes from the above-discussed algorithm for counting MECs of a tree graph.
Let us first define an image of an MEC.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
M
be an MEC of an undirected graph
G
. For
X\subseteq V_{G}
,
O
is said to be an image of
M
on
X
, if
O=M[X]
.

The following corollaries come from
definitions

3.3
and
2.4
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
M
be an MEC of an undirected graph
G
. For any
X\subseteq V_{G}
, there exists a unique image of
M
on
X
.

Proof.

For
X\subseteq V_{G}
, the induced subgraph of
M
on
X
is unique.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
M
be an MEC of an undirected graph
G
, and
O
be an image of
M
on
X\subseteq V_{G}
. Then,
O
is a partial MEC of
G[X]
.

Proof.

From
2.4
, an induced subgraph of an MEC is a partial MEC. Since an image of an MEC is an induced subgraph of it, it is a partial MEC.
∎

We partition the set of MECs
M
of
G
based on its image. Let
\text{MEC}(G,O)
denotes the set of MECs
M
of
G
for which
O
is an image, i.e.,
M[V_{O}]=O
.

From
corollary

3.4
, the image of an MEC
M
of
G
on
X\subseteq V_{G}
is unique. Additionally, from
corollary

3.5
, the image
O
of an MEC
M
of an undirected graph
G
on
X
is a partial MEC of
G[X]
. This implies that for each MEC
M
of
G
, there exists a unique partial MEC
O
of
G[X]
such that
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
. Therefore, the sum of
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
over all partial MECs
O
of
G[X]
is
|\text{MEC}(G)|
, i.e., for any
X\subseteq V_{G}
,

|\text{MEC}(G)|=\sum_{O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])}{|\text{MEC}(G,O)|}

(3)

Equation

3
reduces the problem of counting MECs of
G
into
2

Problem 2
.

Input:
An undirected connected chordal graph
G
, and
X\subseteq V_{G}
.

Output:
Compute
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
for each partial MEC
O
of
G[X]
.

For any chordal graph
G
, we first fix an
X
, and then give a recursive method to solve
2
. In this way, using
eq.

3
, we able to compute
|\text{MEC}(G)|
.
For this, we first construct a clique tree representation (
definition

2.8
)
T
of the chordal graph
G
.
We arbitrarily pick a node
r_{1}
as a root node of
T
.
We give a recursive method to solve
2
for
X=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)
.

We arbitrarily pick a neighbor
r_{2}
of
r_{1}
in
T
. Cut the edge
r_{1}-r_{2}
in
T
. This gives us two induced subgraphs
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
of
T
such that
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the root nodes of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, respectively. For
a\in\{1,2\}
, let
G_{a}
be the induced subgraph of
G
that is represented by
T_{a}
.
We show that if for each
a\in\{1,2\}
, for each
O_{a}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{a}[r_{a}\cup N(r_{a},G_{a})])
, we have the knowledge of
|\text{MEC}(G_{a},O_{a})|
then for each
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])
, we can compute
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
.

We start with finding a relation between the MECs of
G
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
.
The induced subgraphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
of
G
has the following properties: (a)
G_{1}\cup G_{2}=G
, and (b)
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}=r_{1}\cap r_{2}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus{I}
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
(
Blair and Peyton 1993
).
Let
M,M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be the MECs of, respectively,
G,G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
The following observation provides necessary conditions obeyed by images of
M
,
M_{1}
, and
M_{2}
.

Observation 3.6
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
, and
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
. Let
M
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be MECs of
G
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
, respectively. Let
O=M[X]
,
O_{1}=M_{1}[X_{1}]
, and
O_{2}=M_{2}[X_{2}]
. If
(M_{1},M_{2})=\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})
then

1.

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in O_{a}
then
u\rightarrow v\in O
.

2.

For
a\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathcal{V}(O_{a})=\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{a}}])
.

3.

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, if
u-v\in O_{a}
and
u\rightarrow v\in O
then
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
.

Proof of
3.6
.

3.6
follows the following result of
Sharma 2023
.

Lemma 3.7

(Lemma 3.2 of
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph, and let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be subsets of, respectively,
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
M
be an MEC of
G
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
,
O=M[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)]
,
O_{1}=M_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})]
, and
O_{2}=M_{2}[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})]
. Then, for each
a\in\{1,2\}
,

1.

If
u\rightarrow v\in O_{a}
then
u\rightarrow v\in O
.

2.

\mathcal{V}(O_{a})=\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{a}}])
.

3.

If
u-v\in O_{a}
then
u\rightarrow v\in O
if, and only if, either of the following occurs:

(a)

u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
.

(b)

there exists
x-y\in O_{a}
such that
x\rightarrow y\in O
, and there exists a
triangle-free path
from
(x,y)
to
(u,v)
in
M_{a}
with
(x,y)\neq(u,v)
.

(c)

there exists
x-y\in O_{a}
such that
x\rightarrow y\in O
, and there exist
triangle-free paths
from
(x,y)
to
v
, and from
(v,u)
to
x
with
y\neq v
and
u\neq x
.

From the input constructions provided in
3.6
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
,
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the subsets of, respectively,
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
(as they are cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
) such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
.
This shows that the input constraints of
3.6
obey the input constraints of
lemma

3.7
. Therefore, we can use the result of
lemma

3.7
to prove our claims in
3.6
.

Items

1
and
2
of

lemma

3.7
verifies, respectively,
items

1
and
2
of
3.6
. Correctness of
item

3
of
3.6
comes from the fact that when
G,G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
are chordal, and
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are cliques,
items

3a
and
3b
of
lemma

3.7
implies
item

3a
of
lemma

3.7
. This further implies that
item

3
of
lemma

3.7
implies
item

3
of
3.6
. The complete proof of
item

3
of
3.6
is provided in the Supplementary Material.
∎

We define the following to summarize the necessary conditions provided by
3.6
.

Definition 3.8

(
Extension
)
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
, and
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
. Let
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])
,
O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])
, and
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])
. We call
O
as an extension of
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, denoted as
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
, if

1.

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in O_{a}
then
u\rightarrow v\in O
.

2.

For
a\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathcal{V}(O_{a})=\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{a}}])
.

3.

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, for
u-v\in O_{a}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in O
then
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
.

Since
O
,
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
used in
3.6
are partial MECs of, respectively,
G[X]
,
G_{1}[X_{1}]
, and
G_{2}[X_{2}]
(from
2.4
). And, they obey
items

1
,
2
and
3
of
definition

3.8
. Therefore, we can summarize
3.6
as follows:

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
, and
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
. Let
M
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be MECs of
G
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
, respectively. Let
O=M[X]
,
O_{1}=M_{1}[X_{1}]
, and
O_{2}=M_{2}[X_{2}]
. If
(M_{1},M_{2})=\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})
then
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
.

We now show that the necessary conditions that are obeyed by the partial MECs
O,O_{1}
, and
O_{2}
in
lemma

3.9
are sufficient to answer whether there exists an MEC
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
for any pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
, and
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
. Let
O,O_{1}
, and
O_{2}
be partial MECs of, respectively,
G[X]
,
G_{1}[X_{1}]
, and
G_{2}[X_{2}]
such that
O
is an extension of
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, i.e.,
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
.
Then, for each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
, there exists a unique MEC
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Proof of
lemma

3.10
.

Let
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
. We construct an MEC
M
such that
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. Later we show the uniqueness of
M
.
Our construction of
M
starts with the Markov union (
definition

2.13
) of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
and
O
. Since the precondition for the Markov union of graphs is that the graphs need to be pairwise synchronous, we demonstrate that
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
O
satisfy the pairwise synchronous condition.

Observation 3.11
.

M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
O
are synchronous graphs.

Proof.

From the construction,
V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{O}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, and
V_{M_{2}}\cap V_{O}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
. Since
O
is an extension of
O_{1}=M_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})]
and
O_{2}=M_{2}[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})]
, from
definitions

3.8
and
2.12
,
O
and
M_{1}
, and
O
and
M_{2}
are pairwise synchronous.

Suppose
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are not synchronous. Then, there must exist nodes
u,v\in V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{M_{2}}=r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
such that
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
and
v\rightarrow u\in M_{2}
. Since
O_{1}=M_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})]
, and
O_{2}=M_{2}[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})]
,
u\rightarrow v\in O_{1}
and
v\rightarrow u\in O_{2}
. But, then
O
cannot be an extension of
O
as it must violate
item

1
of
definition

3.8
. This leads to a contradiction, as
O
is an extension of
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
. This implies
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are synchronous. This completes the proof of
3.11
.
∎

We construct the required MEC
M
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
, Markov union (
definition

2.13
) of
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
O
.

2.

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, for each undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
,
if
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}\neq\varnothing
then
we do the following:

(a)

Construct an LBFS ordering
\tau
of
\mathcal{C}
that starts with
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}
.

(b)

For
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\tau(u)<\tau(v)
, and
v\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{i}\cup N(r_{i},G_{i})}
, if
u-v\in M
then replace
u-v
with
u\rightarrow v
in
M
if one of the following occurs:

i.

There exists an induced subgraph of the form
w\rightarrow u-v\in M
such that
\tau(w)<\tau(v)
.

ii.

There exists an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow w\rightarrow v-u\in M
such that
\tau(w)<\tau(v)
.

We now prove that
M
is the required MEC. For this, we prove the following: (a) the skeleton of
M
is
G
(
3.12
), (b)
M[V_{O}]=O
(
3.13
), (c)
M
is an MEC (
3.14
), (d)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
(
3.19
), (e)
M
is unique (
3.20
).
We start with the first one.

Observation 3.12
.

The skeleton of
M
is
G
.

Proof.

From the initialization step (step
1
of the construction of
M
),
\textup{skeleton}({M})=\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})\cup\textup{skeleton}({M_{2}})\cup\textup{skeleton}({O})=G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G]=G
. At step
2
of the construction of
M
, we only update
M
by adding an orientation to an undirected edge. This does not change the skeleton. Therefore, the skeleton of
M
is
G
.
∎

We now show the second one.

Observation 3.13
.

M[V_{O}]=O
.

Proof.

From
3.12
, the skeleton of
M
is
G
. This implies
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{O}]})=G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)]=\textup{skeleton}({O})
. Therefore, to prove
M[V_{O}]=O
, we only need to prove that if
u\rightarrow v\in O
then
u\rightarrow v\in M
, and if
u-v\in O
then
u-v\in M
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in O
. Then, from
definition

2.13
,
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. This implies at step
1
of the construction of
M
, we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
. Since, at the second step of the construction of
M
, we do not change any directed edge of
M
,
u\rightarrow v\in M
.

Suppose
u-v\in O
. This implies
u,v\in V_{O}=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
. We first show that at step
1
of the construction of
M
, we have
u-v\in M
.
Since
r_{1}\cap r_{2}
is a vertex separator of
G
, either
u,v\in r_{1}\cap N(r_{1},G_{1})
or
u,v\in r_{2}\cap N(r_{1},G_{1})
. If
u,v\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
then from
item

1
of
definition

3.8
,
u-v\in O_{1}
. Since
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
,
u-v\in M_{1}
. And, if
u,v\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
then
u-v\notin\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})
.
Similarly, if
u,v\in r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
then from
item

1
of
definition

3.8
,
u-v\in O_{2}
. Since
O_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
,
u-v\in M_{2}
. And, if
u,v\notin r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
then
u-v\notin\textup{skeleton}({M_{2}})
.
From
definition

2.13
, this implies
u-v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
.
This further implies that at
item

1
of the construction of
M
, we have
u-v\in M
. Since
item

2
of the construction of
M
does not change an undirected edge with endpoints in
r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
, this implies that
u-v\in M
.

The above discussion implies that
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This completes the proof of
3.13
.
∎

We now prove the third one.

Observation 3.14
.

M
is an MEC.

Proof.

Theorem

2.2
provides necessary and sufficient conditions for a graph to be an MEC.
3.15
,
3.16
,
3.17
and
3.18
show that
M
obeys all the conditions of

theorem

2.2
.

Observation 3.15
.

M
is a chain graph.

Observation 3.16
.

The undirected connected components of
M
are chordal.

Observation 3.17
.

M
does not have an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
.

Observation 3.18
.

The directed edges of
M
are strongly protected.

From
theorem

2.2
,
3.15
,
3.16
,
3.17
and
3.18
implies that
M
is an MEC. Proofs of
3.15
,
3.16
,
3.17
and
3.18
are provided in the Supplementary material. This completes the proof of
3.14
.
∎

The following observations show that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are the projections of
M
on, respectively,
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
, and
M
is unique. Proofs are available in the Supplementary Material.

Observation 3.19
.

\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Observation 3.20
.

There exists a unique
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

This completes the proof of
lemma

3.10
.
∎

Lemma

3.21
uses
lemmas

3.9
and
3.10
to construct a formula that computes
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
for any partial MEC
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)])
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
, and
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
. Then, for any
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])
,

|\text{MEC}(G,O)|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])\\
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])\\
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})\end{subarray}}{|\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})|\times|\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})|}.

(4)

Using
lemma

3.21
, knowing
|\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})|
for each
O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})])
, and
|\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})|
for each
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})])
, we can compute
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
for each
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G])
.
But, for the recursion, we require to compute
|\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})|
for each
O^{\prime}\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])
.

We use the following lemma to evaluate
|\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})|
for any
O^{\prime}\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])
, when we have the knowledge of
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
for all
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)])
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\subseteq V_{G}
. Then, for any
O^{\prime}\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])
,

|\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])\\
O^{\prime}=O[X^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}{|\text{MEC}(G,O)|}.

(5)

Proof sketch of
lemma

3.22
.

For any MEC
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})
, there exists
O=M[X]
. Since
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
,
O^{\prime}=O[X^{\prime}]
. And, for any MEC
M
with induced subgraph
O
such that
O^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph of
O
,
O^{\prime}
is also an induced subgraph of
M
, i.e.,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemmas

3.21
and
3.22
imply the following:

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
,
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
, and
X^{\prime}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)
. Then, for any
O^{\prime}\in\text{PMEC}(G[X^{\prime}])
,

|\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])\\
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])\\
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])\\
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})\\
O^{\prime}=O[X]\end{subarray}}{|\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})|\times|\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})|}.

(6)

3.2.1
Algorithm for counting MECs of a chordal graph

We use
lemma

3.23
to construct a recursive algorithm (
algorithm

2
) that for inputs
G
, a clique tree representation
T
of
G
, and a node
r_{1}
of
T
, outputs a function
f
such that for
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])
,
f(O)=|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
.
Lemma

3.24
provides the correctness of
algorithm

2
.

Input :
A connected chordal graph
G
, a clique tree representation
T
of
G
, a node
r_{1}\in V_{T}
.

Output :
A function
f:\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})])\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that for
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])
,
f(O)=|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
.

1

Create a function
f:\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})])\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

2

foreach

O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})])

do

3

f(O)\leftarrow 0

end foreach

4

5

if

degree of
r_{1}
in
T
= 0

then

6

f(G)=1

7

return

f

end if

8

9

Pick an arbitrary edge
r_{1}-r_{2}\in E_{T}

10

Cut the edge
r_{1}-r_{2}
of
T

11

T_{1}\leftarrow
induced subtree of
T
containing
r_{1}

12

T_{2}\leftarrow
induced subtree of
T
containing
r_{2}

13

G_{1}\leftarrow
induced subgraph of
G
represented by
T_{1}

14

G_{2}\leftarrow
induced subgraph of
G
represented by
T_{2}

15

f_{1}\leftarrow
Count
(G_{1},T_{1},r_{1})

16

f_{2}\leftarrow
Count
(G_{2},T_{2},r_{2})

17

foreach

O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})])

do

18

foreach

O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})])

do

19

foreach

O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)])

do

20

if

O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})

then

21

O^{\prime}\leftarrow O[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]

22

f(O^{\prime})=f(O^{\prime})+f_{1}(O_{1})\times f_{2}(O_{2})

end if

23

end foreach

24

end foreach

25

end foreach

26

27

return

f

Algorithm 2

Count(
G,T,r_{1}
)

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph,
T
be a clique tree representation of
G
, and
r_{1}
be a node of
T
. For input
G,T
and
r_{1}
,
algorithm

2
outputs a function
f:\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that for
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])
,
f(O)=|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
.

Input :
A connected chordal graph
G

Output :

|MEC(G)|

1

Construct a clique tree representation
T
of
G
.

2

Pick an arbitrary node
r_{1}
of
T
as root node.

3

f\leftarrow
Count
(G,T,r_{1})

4

sum\leftarrow 0

5

foreach

O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)])

do

6

sum=sum+
f[O]

end foreach

7

8

return
sum;

Algorithm 3

Count-MEC(
G
)

We use
algorithms

2
and
3
to construct
algorithm

3
that for an input chordal graph
G
, outputs
|\text{MEC}(G)|
.
Lemma

3.25
provides the correctness of
algorithm

3
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph. For input
G
,
algorithm

3
outputs
|\text{MEC}(G)|
.

4
Time Complexity

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
G
be a tree graph. For input graph
G
, and a node
r\in V_{G}
, the time complexity of
algorithm

1
is
O(d^{2}n)
, where
d
is the degree of
G
, and
n
is the number of nodes of
G
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph,
T
be a tree decomposition of
G
, and
r
be a node of
T
. For input
G
,
T
, and
r
, the time complexity of
algorithm

2
is
O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2}))
, where
d
is the degree of graph,
k
is the treewidth of
T
, and
n
is the number of nodes in
G
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For input chordal graph
G
, the time complexity to run
algorithm

3
is
O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2}))
, where
d
is the degree of graph,
k
is the treewidth of
T
, and
n
is the number of nodes in
G
.

Theorem 4.4
.

For a tree graph
G
, we can count the MECs of
G
in
O(d^{2}n)
time, where
n
is the number of nodes in
G
, and
d
is the degree of
G
.

Theorem 4.5
.

For a chordal graph
G
, there exists a fixed parameter tractable algorithm that can count the MECs of
G
in
O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2}))
, where
n
is the number of nodes of
G
, and the parameters
d
and
k
are, respectively, the degree and the treewidth of
G
.

5
Supplementary Material

5.1
Omitted proofs of
section

3.1

Proof of
lemma

3.2
.

From
2.6
, for any MEC
M
of
G
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})
is unique.
For each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})\in\text{MEC}(G_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2})
, we find the MECs
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
We then classify these MECs
M
into
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
,
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,0),\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,1),\ldots,\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,\delta)
(recall
\delta
is the degree of
r_{1}
in
G
).
We further count the MECs in each class. This gives us the required numbers
n_{1}(G,r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{0}(G,r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{1}(G,r_{1})
,
\ldots
,
n_{0}^{\delta}(G,r_{1})
.

For a pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})\in\text{MEC}(G_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2})
,
5.1
provides us with necessary and sufficient conditions for an MEC
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Observation 5.1
.

Let
M,M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be the MECs of
G,G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, respectively. Then,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
if, and only if,

1.

M
contains all the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
.

2.

Either all the v-structures of
M
that are neither v-structures in
M_{1}
nor in
M_{2}
contain an edge
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
, or all the v-structures of
M
that are neither v-structures in
M_{1}
nor in
M_{2}
contain an edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
.

Proof.

We first prove
\leftarrow
of
5.1
. Suppose
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
Since
M_{1}
is a projection of
M
on
V_{G_{1}}
, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})=\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. Similarly,
\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. This proves
item

1
.

From the structure of
M
, each edge of
M
has either both endpoints in
V_{G_{1}}
, or both endpoints in
V_{G_{2}}
, or the endpoints are
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.
If both the edges of the v-structure have endpoints in
V_{G_{1}}
then the v-structure must be in
M_{1}
since
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
. Similarly, if both the edges of the v-structure have endpoints in
V_{G_{2}}
then the v-structure must be in
M_{2}
.
From the construction, there cannot be a v-structure in
M
such that one edge with endpoints in
V_{G_{1}}
and another edge with endpoints in
V_{G_{2}}
, as there is no vertex common in between
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
. Thus, for a v-structure of
M
that is neither in
M_{1}
nor in
M_{2}
, the only option that remains is one edge of the v-structure has endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. Since the edges of a v-structure are directed edges, a v-structure of
M
that is neither in
M_{1}
nor in
M_{2}
contains either
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
or contains
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
.
Also, since
M
cannot have both
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
and
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
. Thus, either all the v-structures of
M
that are neither in
M_{1}
nor in
M_{2}
contain
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
, or all the v-structures of
M
that are neither in
M_{1}
nor in
M_{2}
contain
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
. This proves
item

2
.
This completes the proof of
\leftarrow
of
5.1
.

We now prove
\rightarrow
of
5.1
. Suppose
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
. Since
V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}=\varnothing
, and both
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are neither in
V_{G_{1}}
nor in
V_{G_{2}}
, from
items

1
and
2
of
5.1
,
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Then, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
∎

For each pair of MECs
(M_{1},M_{2})\in\text{MEC}(G_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2})
, we now find MECs
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, i.e.,
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
5.1
.

(M_{1},M_{2})
can be of four kinds: either (a)
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, or (b)
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
(recall that
\delta_{2}
is the degree of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}
), or (c) for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
(recall that
\delta_{1}
is the degree of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}
), or (d) for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
and for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
.
lemma

5.2
deals with the first possibility.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
. Then,

1.

For any MEC
M
of
G
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then one of the following occurs:

(a)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\bigcup_{x\rightarrow r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}}{x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}}

(7)

(b)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\bigcup_{y\rightarrow r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}}{y\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}}

(8)

2.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

7
. Also,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

3.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

8
. Also,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Proof.

We first prove
item

1
of
lemma

5.2
.

Proof of
item

1
of
lemma

5.2
.

Let
M
be an MEC of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
From
5.1
,
M
has the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, and additionally, it can only have the v-structures that involve the edge with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.
Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there exists a node
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. As
M_{1}
is an MEC, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, all the edges adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
are directed. This implies that we can partition the set of neighbors of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}
into
X
and
X^{\prime}
such that
X=\{x:x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}\}
and
X^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\leftarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}\}
. Similarly, as
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, we can partition the set of neighbors of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}
into
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
such that
Y=\{y:y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}\}
and
Y^{\prime}=\{y^{\prime}:y^{\prime}\leftarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}\}
.
According to
lemma

2.7
,
M
has edges
x\rightarrow r_{1}
for
x\in X
,
r_{1}\rightarrow x^{\prime}
for
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
,
y\rightarrow r_{2}
for
y\in Y
, and
r_{2}\rightarrow y^{\prime}
for
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
.
Based on
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in E_{M}
or
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in E_{M}
, otherwise,
M
has an induced subgraph of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}-r_{2}
for
x\in X
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. If
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in E_{M}
, then, in addition to the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M
has the v-structures of the form
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
for
x\in X
, i.e.,
M
obeys
\mathcal{V}(M)
as stated in
eq.

7
. Similarly, if
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in E_{M}
, then
M
obeys
eq.

8
. This completes the proof of
item

1
.
∎

We now prove
items

2
and
3
of
lemma

5.2
.
As discussed in the introduction, there cannot be two distinct MECs with the same skeleton and the same set of v-structures.
This means there can be at most one MEC of
G
containing the v-structures in
eq.

7
, and at most one MEC of
G
containing the v-structures in
eq.

8
.
The uniqueness of MECs present in
items

2
and
3
of
lemma

5.2
comes from this fact.
We will show that
M_{x}=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
is the MEC of
G
that obeys
eq.

7
, and
M_{y}=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
is the MEC of
G
that obeys
eq.

8
.
Let’s begin with
M_{x}
.

To prove
item

2
, we need to demonstrate that (a)
M_{x}
obeys
eq.

7
, (b)
M_{x}
is an MEC of
G
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M_{x},V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M_{x}\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. Let’s first establish that
M_{x}
obeys
eq.

7
.

From the construction of
M_{x}
, the induced subgraph of
M_{x}
on
V_{G_{1}}
is
M_{1}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{V}(M_{x}[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
. Similarly,
\mathcal{V}(M_{x}[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. This implies that
M_{x}
contains the v-structure of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, and in addition, the v-structures it contains must have one edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
. Considering the construction of
M_{x}
, if
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, then either
x=r_{2}
or
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. Therefore, the v-structures of
M_{x}
with one edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
must be of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}
for
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. This demonstrates that the v-structures contained in
M_{x}
obey
eq.

7
.

Now, let’s verify that
M_{x}
is an MEC of
G
. Given the construction of
M_{x}
, its skeleton is
G
, so we only need to establish that
M_{x}
is an MEC. As the skeleton of
M_{x}
is a tree graph, we demonstrate that it satisfies
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
M_{x}
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M_{x}
, there are 3 possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
. If
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
then from the construction of
M_{x}
,
a\rightarrow b-c\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, this contradicts
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. This implies that this case cannot occur. Similarly, there cannot be
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. If
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
then from the construction of
M_{x}
,
r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there must exist a node
x
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. This implies
M_{1}
contains an induced subgraph of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. As seen earlier, this cannot occur as
M_{1}
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. This implies
M_{x}
cannot have any induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. This shows that
M_{x}
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that each directed edge
u\rightarrow v
in
M_{x}
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Let
u\rightarrow v
be a directed edge in
M_{x}
. From the construction of
M_{x}
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, or
u=r_{2}
and
v=r_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then it must be part of an induced subgraph of the form either
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
(similar to
fig.

1
.a), or
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
. From the construction of
M
, the induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Thus, in
M_{x}
,
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Similarly, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
then in
M_{x}
,
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. We now go through the final possibility when
u=r_{2}
and
v=r_{1}
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there exist a node
x\in V_{G_{1}}
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. From the construction of
M_{x}
,
x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{x}
. This implies
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. This shows all the directed edges of
M_{x}
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
The above discussion implies that
M_{x}
is an MEC of
G
that obeys
eq.

7
. We now prove that
\mathcal{P}(M_{x},V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Since
M_{x}
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
5.1
, therefore, from
5.1
,
\mathcal{P}(M_{x},V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. We now prove the remaining part of
item

2
, that is
M_{x}\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. The following observation proves this.

Observation 5.3
.

Let
M
and
M_{1}
be MECs of
G
and
G_{1}
, respectively, such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}})=M_{1}
, and
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
. Then,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

Proof.

Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there exists an
x
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is a projection of
M
, from
lemma

2.7
,
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
. This implies
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.
∎

This completes the proof of
item

2
of
lemma

5.2
. Similarly, we can prove
item

3
of
lemma

5.2
. This concludes the proof of
lemma

5.2
.
∎

Lemma

5.2
implies the following corollary:

Corollary 5.4
.

For
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, the number of MECs
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
is two. Specifically, both MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

Proof.

From
item

1
of
lemma

5.2
, for any MEC
M
of
G
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then
M
has to obey either
eq.

7
or
eq.

8
.

From
item

2
of
lemma

5.2
, there exists a unique MEC that satisfies
eq.

7
, belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. Additionally, from
item

3
of
lemma

5.2
, another unique MEC, following
eq.

8
, also belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

Hence, the total count of MECs is two, and both of them belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.
∎

We now consider the second possibility where
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
. Then:

1.

For any MEC
M
of
G
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then one of the following occurs:

(a)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\bigcup_{x\rightarrow r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}}{x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}}

(9)

(b)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})

(10)

(c)

For some undirected edge
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
,

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}

(11)

2.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

9
. Additionally,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

3.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

10
. Furthermore,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

4.

For each
y
such that
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
, there exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

11
. Also,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Proof.

We first prove
item

1
of
lemma

5.5
.

Proof of
item

1
of
lemma

5.5
.

Let
M
be an MEC of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
From
5.1
,
M
has the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. In addition to those, it can only have v-structures that involve an edge with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.

Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there exists an
x\in V_{G_{1}}
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M_{1}
. Following
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow r_{1}-y
in
M_{1}
. Therefore, each edge adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
is directed. Consequently, we can partition the neighbors of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}
into
X
and
X^{\prime}
such that
X=\{x:x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}\}
and
X^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}:r_{1}\rightarrow x^{\prime}\in M_{1}\}
with
X
being non-empty.

From
lemma

2.7
, for
x\in X
,
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, and for
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
,
r_{1}\rightarrow x^{\prime}\in M
. Further, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
or
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, otherwise, for some
x\in X
,
M
contains an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow r_{1}-r_{2}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
..

If
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, then the v-structures with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are
x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}
for each
x\in X
. In this case, the v-structures of
M
obey
eq.

9
.

Suppose
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
, there cannot be an edge
y\rightarrow r_{2}
in
M_{2}
. This implies that we can partition the neighbors of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}
into
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
such that
Y=\{y:y-r_{2}\in M_{2}\}
is a set of
j
elements (from the definition of
\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
), and
Y^{\prime}=\{y^{\prime}:r_{2}\rightarrow y^{\prime}\in M_{2}\}
is a set of
\delta_{2}-j
elements.
Since
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, according to
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, for
y\in Y
, either
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
or
r_{2}\rightarrow y\in M
, otherwise,
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}-y
is an induced subgraph of
M
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.
If for two distinct
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
,
y_{1}\rightarrow r_{2},y_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, then we get a v-structure
y_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y_{2}
in
M
such the nodes of the v-structure are in
V_{G_{2}}
, but the v-structure is not in
M_{2}
, implying
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])\neq\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
, which further implies
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{2}})\neq M_{2}
, a contradiction. This implies that there exists at most one
y\in Y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
.

If there does not exist any
y\in Y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, then there won’t be any v-structure in
M
with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. In this case,
M
only contains the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, and obeys
eq.

10
.

However, if there exists a
y\in Y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, then
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
is the only v-structure with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
in
M
. In this case,
M
obeys
eq.

11
.

We show that in each possibility,
M
obeys
item

1
of
lemma

5.5
.
This completes the proof of
item

1
of
lemma

5.5
.
∎

As discussed in the introduction, there cannot be two distinct MECs with the same skeleton and the same set of v-structures. The uniqueness of the MECs present in
items

2
,
3
and
4
of
lemma

5.5
arises from this fact.

We now prove
items

2
,
3
and
4
of
lemma

5.5
.

Proof of
item

2
of
lemma

5.5
.

We construct an MEC of
G
satisfying
item

2
of
lemma

5.5
. Let
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
. To verify that
M
adheres to
item

2
, we need to demonstrate the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
satisfies
eq.

9
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

The proof follows a similar approach to that of verifying
item

2
in
lemma

5.2
. For completeness, we provide the proof.

Firstly, we establish that
M
is an MEC of
G
. The construction of
M
implies that its skeleton is identical to
G
. Hence, we only need to confirm that
M
is an MEC, i.e., it satisfies
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. There are three possible scenarios from the construction of
M
: (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
. If
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, then by construction,
a\rightarrow b-c\in M_{1}
. As
M_{1}
is an MEC, this contradicts
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. This scenario is impossible. Similarly,
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
is not viable.

Considering
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
, from the construction of
M
,
r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. As
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there must exist a node
x
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. This implies
M_{1}
contains an induced subgraph of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. However, this contradicts
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, as
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
. Consequently,
M
cannot contain any induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. Thus,
M
satisfies
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now demonstrate that each directed edge
u\rightarrow v
in
M
adheres to
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Let
u\rightarrow v
be a directed edge in
M
. Based on the construction of
M
, it follows that either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, or
u=r_{2}
and
v=r_{1}
.

Considering
M_{1}
as an MEC of tree graph
G_{1}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, then according to
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph in
M_{1}
, such as either
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
or
w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
. As the induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is also a subset of
M
, when
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
,
u\rightarrow v
satisfies
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
for
M
.

Similarly, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, then
u\rightarrow v
satisfies
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
for
M
. Now, let’s consider the scenario when
u=r_{2}
and
v=r_{1}
. As
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there exists a node
x\in V_{G_{1}}
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. With the construction of
M
,
x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Consequently, in this case,
u\rightarrow v
also fulfills
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Thus, the analysis shows that
M
satisfies
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. The aforementioned discussions imply that
M
complies with
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. Consequently, this verifies that
M
is an MEC.

Next, we demonstrate that the v-structures of
M
adhere to
eq.

9
. Considering the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
represent the induced subgraphs of
M
on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
, respectively. Thus,
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Since
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, apart from the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M
can possess v-structures involving the edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
. Given
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there exists a non-empty set
X=\{x:x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M\}
. For each
x\in X
,
x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}
constitutes a v-structure in
M
. Thus, it is confirmed that the v-structures in
M
satisfy
eq.

9
.

Next, we verify
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. As per the construction of
M
,
M[V_{G_{1}}]=M_{1}
, and
M[V_{G_{2}}]=M_{2}
. Consequently,
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. According to
definition

2.5
, this further indicates
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Referring to
5.3
, we conclude that
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This completes the proof for
item

2
of
lemma

5.5
.
∎

Proof of
item

3
of
lemma

5.5
.

We create an MEC
M
of
G
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
.

2.

Update
M
by replacing
u-v
in
M
with
u\rightarrow v
if
u-v\in M_{2}
and there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
.

We demonstrate that
M
, constructed using the aforementioned steps, adheres to
item

3
of
lemma

5.5
. To prove this, we need to establish the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
adheres to
eq.

10
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

Firstly, we demonstrate that
M
is an MEC, i.e.,
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are three possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
.
One by one, we show that none of the possibilities occurs.

From the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M
with vertices in
V_{G_{1}}
is also an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
(note that
item

2
does not make any change in an edge with the endpoints in
V_{G_{1}}
). Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

1
of

theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v-w\in M_{1}
. This implies that the first possibility cannot occur.

Suppose,
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
such that
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
b-c\in M_{2}
(note that
item

2
does not change a directed edge in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
into an undirected edge), and either
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
or
a-b\in M_{2}
. If
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
then
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph in the MEC
M_{2}
of the tree graph
G_{2}
, contradicting
item

1
of

theorem

3.1
. And, if
a-b\in M_{2}
then it must have been directed at
item

2
. This implies there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(a,b)
in
M_{2}
. This further implies that there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(b,c)
in
M_{2}
. But, then, we have
b\rightarrow c\in M
at
item

2
. This shows that the second possibility also cannot occur.

Suppose
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}-c
in
M
such that
c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, this implies
r_{2}-c\in M_{2}
. But, edge
r_{2}-c
is a path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},c)
. Then, it must have been directed at
item

2
. This implies that the third possibility also cannot occur. This shows that
M
cannot have an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow v-w
. This implies that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
then either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u=r_{1}
and
v=r_{2}
. We show that in each of the possibilities
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, there are two cases: either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
or
u-v\in M_{2}
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
.
Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, a directed induced subgraph of
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore, in the case when
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u-v\in M_{2}
then it must have been directed in
M
at
item

2
. This implies there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
.
Let the path be
P=(x_{0}=r_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1}=u,x_{l}=v)
for some
l\geq 1
. From the construction of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i+1}\in M
because
P_{i}=(x_{0}=r_{2},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1})
is an undirected path in
M_{2}
from
r_{2}
to
(x_{i},x_{i+1})
. If
l>1
then
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x_{l-2}\rightarrow x_{l-1}\rightarrow x_{l}
in
M
.
And, if
l=1
then
r_{2}=u
, and
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\rightarrow v
in
M
.
Thus, we show that if
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
in
M
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, there must exist
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M_{1}
. From the construction of
M
,
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
. Then,
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
is part of an induced subgraph of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
. Thus, we show that each edge of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion shows that
M
is an MEC as it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. From the construction of
M
, the skelton of
M
is
G
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
.

We now show that
M
obeys
eq.

10
. From the construction of
M
, directed edges of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are directed edges of
M
. This implies the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are the v-structures of
M
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. For the completeness of the proof, we show that if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then either it is a v-structure of
M_{1}
or it is a v-structure of
M_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
, i.e.,
M
obeys
eq.

10
.

Suppose there exists a v-structure
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
. We show that either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{1}
, or it is a v-structure in
M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
, there are following possibilities: (a)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
in
V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
.

From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(note that
item

2
does not change the orientation of any edge of
M_{1}
). This implies that if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{1}
.

Suppose
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
in
V_{G_{2}}
. We show that this possibility cannot occur. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0)
, either
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
or
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
. We show that none of them occurs.
Suppose
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
.
From the construction of
M
(
items

1
and
2
), if
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
then
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M
. But, we have
r_{2}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies
r_{2}\rightarrow w\notin M_{2}
.
Suppose
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
.
Then, from
item

2
,
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M
. Again, we have
r_{2}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies that this case also cannot occur.
We now move to the final possibility.

Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
then
a\rightarrow b\in M
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{2}
or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, and either
v-w\in M_{2}
or
v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
. From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
or
u-v-w\in M_{2}
(recall that
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
).
If
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
then the v-structure is a v-structure of
M_{2}
. Suppose
u-v-w\in M_{2}
. Then, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it must have been directed at
item

2
, i.e., there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
.
Since
u-v-w\in M_{2}
, the existence of a path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
implies the existence of a path from
r_{2}
to
(v,w)
in
M_{2}
. Also, since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, there cannot be two different paths from
r_{2}
to
w
.
This implies at
item

2
, if we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
then we also have
v\rightarrow w\in M
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have assumed that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.
This implies there cannot be
u-v-w\in M_{2}
and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
. Thus, we show that in all the possible scenarios, if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then it is a v-structure either in
M_{1}
or in
M_{2}
.
This completes the proof that
M
obeys
eq.

10
.

The above discussion implies that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}]=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}]=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Therefore, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

From
5.3
,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This completes the proof of
item

3
of
lemma

5.5
.
∎

Proof of
item

4
of
lemma

5.5
.

We construct an MEC of
G
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
.

2.

Pick an edge
y-r_{2}\in M_{2}
. Replace the edge
y-r_{2}
in
M
with
y\rightarrow r_{2}
.

3.

Update
M
by replacing
u-v
of
M
with
u\rightarrow v
, if
u-v\in M_{2}
and there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
such that the path does not contain
y
.

We show that
M
constructed using the above steps will obey
item

4
of
lemma

5.5
. To prove this, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

11
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC of
G
. From the construction of
M
, the skeleton of
M
is
G
. Therefore, it would be sufficient to show that
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We first show that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are three possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
.
One by one, we show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M
with vertices in
V_{G_{1}}
is also an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
(note that
items

2
and
3
does not make any change in an edge with the endpoints in
V_{G_{1}}
). Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v-w\in M_{1}
. This implies that the first possibility cannot occur.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
b-c\in M_{2}
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change a directed edge in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
into an undirected edge), and either
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
or
a-b\in M_{2}
. If
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
then
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph in the MEC
M_{2}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. And, if
a-b\in M_{2}
then it must have been directed at either at
item

2
(first case) or at
item

3
(second case). If it has directed at
item

2
then
a=y,b=r_{2}
and
c\neq y
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
