. This implies
r_{2}-c
is a path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},c)
. But, then at
item

3
,
r_{2}-c
has been converted into
r_{2}\rightarrow c
. This implies that the first case cannot occur. We move to the second case when
a-b
has been directed at
item

3
. This implies there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(a,b)
in
M_{2}
. This further implies that there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(b,c)
in
M_{2}
. But, then, we have
b\rightarrow c\in M
at
item

3
. This shows that the second possibility cannot occur.

Suppose
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, this implies
r_{2}-c\in M_{2}
. There are two cases, either
c=y
, or
c\neq y
. If
c=y
, then at
item

2
, we have
c\rightarrow b\in M
. Therefore, this case cannot occur. Suppose
c\neq y
. But, edge
r_{2}-c
is a path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},c)
. Then, it must have been directed at
item

3
. This implies that the third possibility also cannot occur. Thus, we show that
M
cannot have an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow v-w
. This implies that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
then either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u=r_{1}
and
v=r_{2}
. We show that in each of the possibilities
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change the orientation of any edge of
M_{1}
). This implies
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, there are two cases: either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
or
u-v\in M_{2}
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. From the construction of
M
, a directed induced subgraph of
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(as a directed edge of
M_{2}
is a directed edge of
M
, and the skeletons of
M_{2}
and
M[V_{G_{2}}
are the same). Therefore, in this case also
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u-v\in M_{2}
. Then, it must have been directed in
M
either at
item

2
or at
item

3
. Suppose
u-v\in M_{2}
and it gets directed in
M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=y
and
v=r_{2}
. In that case,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
, obeying
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u-v
gets directed in
M
at
item

3
. This implies there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
, and the path does not contain
y
.
Let the path be
P=(x_{0}=r_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1}=u,x_{l}=v)
for some
l\geq 1
. From the construction of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i+1}\in M
because
P_{i}=(x_{0}=r_{2},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1})
is an undirected path in
M_{2}
from
r_{2}
to
(x_{i},x_{i+1})
, and
P_{i}
does not contain
y
either. If
l>1
then
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x_{l-2}\rightarrow x_{l-1}\rightarrow x_{l}
in
M
.
And, if
l=1
then
r_{2}=u
, and
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\rightarrow v
in
M
.
Thus, we show that if
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
in
M
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
in
M
. Thus, we show that each edge of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion shows that
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
.

We now show that
M
obeys
eq.

11
. From the construction of
M
, directed edges of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are directed edges of
M
. Also,
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
is a v-structure in
M
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. For the completeness of the proof, we show that if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then either it is a v-structure of
M_{1}
or it is a v-structure of
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
and
w=y
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}
. This further implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}
, i.e.,
M
obeys
eq.

11
.

Suppose there exists a v-structure
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
. From the construction of
M
, there are following possibilities: (a)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w=y
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
in
V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
.

Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies that if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{1}
.

Suppose
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
in
V_{G_{2}}
. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0)
, either
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
or
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
(
items

1
,
2
and
3
), if
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
then
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M
. But, we have
r_{2}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies that
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
. From
items

2
and
3
, if we have
w\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
then
w=y
, otherwise, we have
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
as
r_{2}-w
is an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},w)
, and the path does not contain
y
. This implies
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w=y
.

Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
then
a\rightarrow b\in M
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{2}
or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, and either
v-w\in M_{2}
or
v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
or
u-v-w\in M_{2}
(recall that
M_{2}
is an MEC).
If
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
then the v-structure is a v-structure of
M_{2}
. Suppose
u-v-w\in M_{2}
. Then, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it must have been directed either at
item

2
or at
item

2
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=y
and
v=r_{2}
. But, then
v-w
is a path from
(r_{2})
to
(v,w)
(and the path does not contain
y
). And, we have
v\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
, a contradiction, as the v-structure implies
w\rightarrow v\in M
. This implies that this case cannot occur. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M
at
item

3
. Then, there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
, and the path does not contain
y
.
Since
u-v-w\in M_{2}
, the existence of a path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
implies the existence of a path from
r_{2}
to
(v,w)
in
M_{2}
, and the new path also does not contain
y
. Also, since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, there cannot be two different paths from
r_{2}
to
w
.
This implies at
item

3
, if we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
then we also have
v\rightarrow w\in M
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have assumed that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.
This implies there cannot be
u-v-w\in M_{2}
and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
. Thus, we show that in all the possible scenarios, if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then it is a v-structure either in
M_{1}
or in
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
and
w=y
.
This completes the proof that
M
obeys
eq.

11
.

The above discussion implies that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Therefore, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

From
5.3
,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This completes the proof of
item

4
of
lemma

5.5
.
∎

This completes the proof of
lemma

5.5
.
∎

Lemma

5.5
implies the following corollary:

Corollary 5.6
.

For
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
,
the number of MECs
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
is j+ 2. More specifically, all the MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

Proof.

From
item

1
of
lemma

5.5
, for any MEC
M
of
G
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then
M
has to obey either
eq.

9
or
eq.

10
or
eq.

11
.

From
item

2
of
lemma

5.5
, there exists a unique MEC that satisfies
eq.

9
, belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. Additionally, from
item

3
of
lemma

5.5
, another unique MEC, following
eq.

10
, also belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. Furthermore, given
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
,
j
instances of
y
exist such that
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
. According to
item

4
of
lemma

5.5
, for each
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
, there exists a unique MEC satisfying
eq.

11
. Consequently, there are
j
MECs following
eq.

11
, all belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
as per
item

4
.

Hence, the total count of MECs is
j+2
, and all of them belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.
∎

We now deal with the third possibility when for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
. Then,

1.

For any
M\in
MEC
(G)
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then one of the following occurs:

(a)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\bigcup_{y\rightarrow r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}}{y\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}}

(12)

(b)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})

(13)

(c)

For some undirected edge
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
,

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}\}

(14)

2.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

12
. Additionally,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

3.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

13
. Furthermore,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

4.

For each
x
such that
x-r_{2}\in E_{M_{1}}
, there exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

14
. Also,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Proof.

We first prove
item

1
of
lemma

5.7
.

Proof of
item

1
of
lemma

5.7
.

Let
M
be an MEC of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
From
5.1
,
M
has the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. Other than that, it can only have v-structures that involve an edge with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.

Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there exists a
y\in V_{G_{2}}
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}
in
M_{2}
.
From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
y\rightarrow r_{2}-y^{\prime}
in
M_{2}
. Therefore, each edge adjacent to
r_{2}
in
M_{2}
is directed. This means we can partition the neighbors of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}
into
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
such that
Y=\{y:y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}\}
and
Y^{\prime}=\{y^{\prime}:r_{2}\rightarrow y^{\prime}\in M_{2}\}
with
Y
being non-empty.
From
lemma

2.7
, for
y\in Y
,
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, and for
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
,
r_{2}\rightarrow y^{\prime}\in M
. From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
or
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, otherwise, for some
y\in Y
,
M
contains an induced subgraph
y\rightarrow r_{2}-r_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
. Then, the v-structures with endpoints
r_{2}
and
r_{1}
are
y\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}
for each
y\in Y
. In this case, the v-structures of
M
obey
eq.

12
.

Suppose
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, there cannot be an edge
x\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M_{1}
. This implies that we can partition the neighbors of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}
into
X
and
X^{\prime}
such that
X=\{x:x-r_{1}\in M_{1}\}
is a set of
i
elements (from the definition of
\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
), and
X^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}:r_{1}\rightarrow x^{\prime}\in M_{1}\}
is a set of
\delta_{1}-i
elements.
Since
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, for
x\in X
, either
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
or
r_{1}\rightarrow x\in M
, otherwise, there exists an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}-x\in M_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.
If for two distinct
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,
x_{1}\rightarrow r_{1},x_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, then we get a v-structure
x_{1}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x_{2}
in
M
such the nodes of the v-structure are in
V_{G_{1}}
, but the v-structure is not in
M_{1}
, implying
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])\neq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, which further implies
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}})\neq M_{1}
, a contradiction.
This implies that there exists at most one
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
. If there does not exist any
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, then there won’t be any v-structure in
M
with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. In this case,
M
only contains the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, and obeys
eq.

13
. If there exists an
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, then
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
is the only v-structure with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
in
M
. In this case,
M
obeys
eq.

14
. This completes the proof of
item

1
of
lemma

5.7
.

∎

As discussed in the introduction, there cannot be two distinct MECs with the same skeleton and the same set of v-structures. Uniqueness of the MECs present in
items

2
,
3
and
4
of
lemma

5.7
comes from this fact.
We now prove
items

2
,
3
and
4
of
lemma

5.7
.

Proof of
item

2
of
lemma

5.7
.

We construct an MEC of
G
that obeys
item

2
of
lemma

5.7
. Let
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
. To prove that
M
obeys
item

2
, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

12
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC of
G
. From the construction of
M
, it is clear that the skeleton of
M
is
G
. Thus, the only thing we have to show is that
M
is an MEC, i.e., it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are 3 possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
. We show that none of these possibilities occurs.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
a\rightarrow b-c\in M_{1}
. This contradicts
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, as
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
. This implies that this possibility cannot occur.
Similarly, there cannot be
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, as from the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is also an induced subgraph of
M
.

Suppose
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
. Then, from the construction of
M
,
r_{2}-c\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there must exist a node
y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}
. This implies
M_{2}
contains an induced subgraph of the form
y\rightarrow r_{2}-c\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, this contradicts
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. This implies this possibility also cannot occur. This further implies that
M
cannot have any induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
.

We now show that each directed edge
u\rightarrow v
of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Let
u\rightarrow v
be a directed edge in
M
. From the construction of
M
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, or
u=r_{1}
and
v=r_{2}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
then from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
or
w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
. From the construction of
M
, the induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Thus, when
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
,
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
for
M
.
Similarly, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
for
M
. We now go through the final possibility when
u=r_{1}
and
v=r_{2}
. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there exist a node
y\in V_{G_{2}}
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
,
y\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies in this case also
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. This shows that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion implies that
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.
This further implies that
M
is an MEC.

We now show that the v-structures of
M
obey
eq.

12
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are the induced subgraphs of
M
on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Since
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, other than the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M
can have the v-structures with an edge
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there exists a non-empty set
Y=\{y:y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}\}
. For each
y\in Y
,
y\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow r_{2}
is a v-structure in
M
. This shows that the v-structures in
M
obey
eq.

12
.

We now show that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. From the construction of
M
,
M[V_{G_{1}}]=M_{1}
, and
M[V_{G_{2}}]=M_{2}
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. From
definition

2.5
, this further implies
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
.
In
M_{1}
,
i
edges adjacent to
r_{1}
are undirected and the remaining
\delta_{1}-i
adjacent edges of
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
are directed outward of
r_{1}
. The only edge that is adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M
but not adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
is
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
, which is directed outward of
r_{1}
. Therefore, no edge adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M
is incoming to
r_{1}
, and
i
undirected edges adjacent to
M
are undirected. This implies
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
. This completes the proof of
item

2
of
lemma

5.7
.
∎

Proof of
item

3
of
lemma

5.7
.

We construct an MEC of
G
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
.

2.

Update
M
by replacing
u-v
of
M
with
u\rightarrow v
, if
u-v\in M_{1}
and there exists an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
.

We show that
M
constructed using the above steps will obey
item

3
of
lemma

5.7
. To prove this, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

13
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC, i.e., it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We first show that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are three possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
.
One by one, we show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose,
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
such that
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
b-c\in M_{1}
(note that
item

2
does not change a directed edge in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
into an undirected edge), and either
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
or
a-b\in M_{1}
. If
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph in the MEC
M_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. And, if
a-b\in M_{1}
then it must have been directed at
item

2
. This implies there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(a,b)
in
M_{1}
. This further implies that there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(b,c)
in
M_{1}
. But, then, we have
b\rightarrow c\in M
at
item

2
. This shows that the first possibility cannot occur.

From the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M
with vertices in
V_{G_{2}}
is also an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
(note that
item

2
does not make any change in an edge with the endpoints in
V_{G_{2}}
). Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v-w\in M_{2}
. This implies that the second possibility cannot occur.

Suppose
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}-c
in
M
such that
c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, this implies
r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. But, edge
r_{1}-c
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},c)
. Then, it must have been directed at
item

2
. This implies that the third possibility also cannot occur. This shows that
M
cannot have an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow v-w
. This implies that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
then either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u=r_{1}
and
v=r_{2}
. We will show that in each of the possibilities,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, there are two cases: either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
u-v\in M_{1}
.
Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
.
Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
, or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. From the construction of
M
, a directed induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph in
M
, as a directed edge in
M_{1}
is a directed edge in
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M
.
When
u-v\in M_{1}
then it must have been directed in
M
at
item

2
. This implies there exists an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
.
Let the path be
P=(x_{0}=r_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1}=u,x_{l}=v)
for some
l\geq 1
. From the construction of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i+1}\in M
because
P_{i}=(x_{0}=r_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1})
is an undirected path in
M_{1}
from
r_{1}
to
(x_{i},x_{i+1})
. If
l>1
then
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x_{l-2}\rightarrow x_{l-1}\rightarrow x_{l}
in
M
.
And, if
l=1
then
r_{1}=u
, and
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\rightarrow v
in
M
. Thus, we show that if
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(note that
item

2
does not change the orientation of any edge of
M_{2}
). This implies
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there must exist
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
,
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
. Then,
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
is part of an induced subgraph of the form
y\rightarrow r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
. Thus, we show that each edge of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion shows that
M
is an MEC as it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. From the construction of
M
, the skelton of
M
is
G
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
.

We now show that
M
obeys
eq.

13
. From the construction of
M
, directed edges of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are directed edges of
M
. This implies the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are the v-structures of
M
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. For the completeness of the proof, we show that if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then either it is a v-structure of
M_{1}
or it is a v-structure of
M_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
, i.e.,
M
obeys
eq.

13
.

Suppose there exists a v-structure
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
. We show that either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{1}
, or it is a v-structure in
M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
, there are following possibilities: (a)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
in
V_{G_{1}}
, or (c)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
.

Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then
a\rightarrow b\in M
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, and either
v-w\in M_{1}
or
v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
. From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
or
u-v-w\in M_{1}
(recall that
M_{1}
is an MEC).
If
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
then the v-structure is a v-structure of
M_{1}
. Suppose
u-v-w\in M_{1}
. Then, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it must have been directed at
item

2
, i.e., there exists an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
.
Since
u-v-w\in M_{1}
, the existence of a path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
implies the existence of a path from
r_{1}
to
(v,w)
in
M_{1}
. Also, since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, there cannot be two different paths from
r_{1}
to
w
.
This implies at
item

2
, if we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
then we also have
v\rightarrow w\in M
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have assumed that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.
This implies there cannot be
u-v-w\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.

Suppose
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
in
V_{G_{1}}
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0)
, either
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M_{1}
or
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
(
items

1
and
2
), if
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M_{1}
then
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M
. But, we have
r_{1}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies that
r_{1}-w\in M_{1}
. But, then, from
item

2
,
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M
. This implies that this case cannot occur.

We now move to the final possibility, when
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies that if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{2}
. Thus, we show that in all the possible scenarios, if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then it is a v-structure either in
M_{1}
or in
M_{2}
.
This completes the proof that
M
obeys
eq.

13
.

The above discussion implies that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Therefore, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Since there is an incoming edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M
,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This completes the proof of
item

3
of
lemma

5.7
.
∎

Proof of
item

4
of
lemma

5.7
.

We construct an MEC
M
of
G
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
.

2.

Pick an edge
x-r_{1}\in M_{1}
. Replace the edge
x-r_{1}
in
M
with
x\rightarrow r_{1}
.

3.

Update
M
by replacing
u-v
of
M
with
u\rightarrow v
, if
u-v\in M_{1}
and there exists an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
such that the path does not contain
x
.

We show that
M
constructed using the above steps will obey
item

4
of
lemma

5.7
. To prove this, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

14
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC i.e., it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We first show that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are three possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
.
One by one, we show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
b-c\in M_{1}
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change a directed edge in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
into an undirected edge), and either
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
or
a-b\in M_{1}
. If
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph in the MEC
M_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. And, if
a-b\in M_{1}
then it must have been directed at either at
item

2
(first case) or at
item

3
(second case). If it has been directed at
item

2
then
a=x,b=r_{1}
and
c\neq x
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
. This implies
r_{1}-c
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},c)
. But, then at
item

3
,
r_{1}-c
has been converted into
r_{1}\rightarrow c
. This implies that the first case cannot occur. We move to the second case when
a-b
has been directed at
item

3
. This implies there exists a path from
r_{1}
to
(a,b)
in
M_{1}
. This further implies that there exists a path from
r_{1}
to
(b,c)
in
M_{1}
. But, then, we have
b\rightarrow c\in M
at
item

3
, a contradiction (as from our assumption
c\rightarrow b
is part of the v-structure of
M
).
This implies that the first possibility cannot occur.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M
with vertices in
V_{G_{2}}
is also an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
(note that
items

2
and
3
does not make any change in an edge with the endpoints in
V_{G_{2}}
). Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v-w\in M_{2}
. This shows that the second possibility cannot occur.

Suppose
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, this implies
r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. There are two cases, either
c=x
, or
c\neq x
. If
c=x
, then at
item

2
, we have
c\rightarrow b\in M
. Therefore, this case cannot occur. Suppose
c\neq x
. But, edge
r_{1}-c
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},c)
. Then, it must have been directed at
item

3
. This implies that the third possibility also cannot occur. This shows that
M
cannot have an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow v-w
. This implies that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
then either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u=r_{2}
and
v=r_{1}
. We show that in each of the possibilities
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, there are two cases: either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
or
u-v\in M_{1}
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
.
Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
, or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. From the construction of
M
, a directed induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph in
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M
.

Suppose
u-v\in M_{1}
. Then,
u-v
must have been directed in
M
either at
item

2
or at
item

3
. Suppose
u-v\in M_{1}
and it gets directed in
M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=x
and
v=r_{2}
. In that case,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
, obeying
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
Suppose
u-v\in M_{1}
and it gets directed in
M
at
item

3
. This implies there exists an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
, and the path does not contain
x
.
Let the path be
P=(x_{0}=r_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1}=u,x_{l}=v)
for some
l\geq 1
. From the construction of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i+1}\in M
because
P_{i}=(x_{0}=r_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1})
is an undirected path in
M_{1}
from
r_{1}
to
(x_{i},x_{i+1})
, and
P_{i}
does not contain
x
either. If
l>1
then
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x_{l-2}\rightarrow x_{l-1}\rightarrow x_{l}
in
M
.
And, if
l=1
then
r_{1}=u
, and
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\rightarrow v
in
M
.
Thus, we show that if
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change the orientation of any edge of
M_{2}
). This implies
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
in
M
. Thus, we show that each edge of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion shows that
M
is an MEC as it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. From the construction of
M
, the skelton of
M
is
G
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
.

We now show that
M
obeys
eq.

14
. From the construction of
M
, directed edges of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are directed edges of
M
. Also,
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
is a v-structure in
M
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. For the completeness of the proof, we show that if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then either it is a v-structure of
M_{1}
or it is a v-structure of
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w=x
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x\}
. This further implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x\}
, i.e.,
M
obeys
eq.

14
.

Suppose there exists a v-structure
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
. From the construction of
M
, there are following possibilities: (a)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
in
V_{G_{1}}
, or (c)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
.

Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then
a\rightarrow b\in M
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{1}
or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, and either
v-w\in M_{1}
or
v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
. From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
or
u-v-w\in M_{1}
.
If
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
then the v-structure is a v-structure of
M_{1}
. Suppose
u-v-w\in M_{1}
. Then, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it must have been directed either at
item

2
or at
item

3
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M
has been directed at
item

2
. Then,
u=x
and
v=r_{1}
. But, then
v-w
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(v,w)
(and the path does not contain
x
). And, we have
v\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
, a contradiction, as the v-structure implies
w\rightarrow v\in M
. This implies that this case cannot occur.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M
has been directed at
item

3
. Then, there exists a path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
, and the path does not contain
x
.
Since
u-v-w\in M_{1}
, the existence of a path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
implies the existence of a path from
r_{1}
to
(v,w)
in
M_{1}
, and the new path also does not contain
x
. Also, since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, there cannot be two different paths from
r_{1}
to
w
.
This implies at
item

2
, if we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
then we also have
v\rightarrow w\in M
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have assumed that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.
This implies there cannot be
u-v-w\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.

Suppose
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
in
V_{G_{1}}
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0)
, either
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M_{1}
or
r_{1}-w\in M_{1}
. From the construction of
M
(
items

1
,
2
and
3
), if
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M_{1}
then
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M
. But, we have
r_{1}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies that
r_{1}-w\in M_{1}
. From
items

2
and
3
, if we have
w\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
then
w=x
, otherwise, we have
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
as
r_{1}-w
is an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},w)
, and the path does not contain
x
. This implies
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w=x
.

We now move to the final possibility. Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies that if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{2}
.
Thus, we show that in all the possible scenarios, if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then it is a v-structure either in
M_{1}
or in
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
and
w=x
.
This completes the proof that
M
obeys
eq.

14
.

The above discussion implies that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Therefore, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Since there is an incoming edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M
,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This completes the proof of
item

4
of
lemma

5.7
.
∎

This completes the proof of
lemma

5.7
.
∎

Lemma

5.7
implies the following corollary:

Corollary 5.8
.

For
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
,
the number of MECs
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
is i+ 2. More specifically,
i+1
MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and one MEC belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
.

Proof.

From
item

1
of
lemma

5.7
, for any MEC
M
of
G
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then
M
has to obey either
eqs.

12
,
13
and
14
.
From
item

2
of
lemma

5.7
, there exists a unique MEC that satisfies
eq.

12
, belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
. As indicated in
item

3
of
lemma

5.7
, another unique MEC, following
eq.

13
, belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

Given
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
,
i
instances of
x
exist such that
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
. According to
item

4
of
lemma

5.7
, for each
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
, there exists a unique MEC satisfying
eq.

14
. Consequently, there are
i
MECs following
eq.

14
, all belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
as per
item

3
.

Hence, the total count of MECs is
i+2
. Out of which,
i+1
MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and one MEC belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
.
∎

We now deal with the last possibility, when for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, and for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)

Lemma 5.9
.

Let for some
0\leq i\leq\delta_{1}
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, and for some
0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}
,
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
.

1.

Let
M\in
MEC
(G)
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. Then,
one of the following occurs:

(a)

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})

(15)

(b)

For some undirected edge
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
,

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{x\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow r_{2}\}

(16)

(c)

For some undirected edge
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
,

\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{y\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}\}

(17)

2.

There exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

15
. Additionally,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i+1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

3.

For each
x
such that
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
, there exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

16
. Furthermore,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

4.

For each
y
such that
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
, there exists a unique MEC
M
of
G
that obeys
eq.

17
. Also,
M
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
, and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Proof.

We first prove
item

1
of
lemma

5.9
.

Proof of
item

1
of
lemma

5.9
.

Let
M
be an MEC of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.
From
5.1
,
M
has the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. Other than that, it can only have the v-structures that involve an edge with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. There are three possibilities: either
r_{1}-r_{2}\in M
or
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
or
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
.

Suppose
r_{1}-r_{2}\in M
. Then, there cannot be any v-structure with an edge with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, because a v-structure contains directed edges, and
r_{1}-r_{2}
is undirected. This implies that if
r_{1}-r_{2}\in M
then the only v-structures
M
can have are the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, i.e.,
M
follows
eq.

15
.

Suppose
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
.
Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, we can partition the set of neighbors of
r_{1}
in
G_{1}
into
X
and
X^{\prime}
such that
X=\{x:x-r_{1}\in M_{1}\}
is a set of
i
nodes, and
X^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}:r_{1}\rightarrow x^{\prime}\in M_{1}\}
of
\delta_{1}-i
nodes. From
lemma

2.7
, for
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
,
r_{1}\rightarrow x^{\prime}\in M
. Since
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, for
x\in X
, either
r_{1}\rightarrow x\in M
or
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, otherwise,
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}-x
is an induced subgraph in
M
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.
If for two distinct
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,
x_{1}\rightarrow r_{1},x_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
, then we get a v-structure
x_{1}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x_{2}
in
M
such the nodes of the v-structure are in
V_{G_{1}}
, but the v-structure is not in
M_{1}
, implying
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])\neq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, which further implies
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}})\neq M_{1}
, a contradiction. This implies that there exists at most one
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
. If there is no
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
then there won’t be any v-structure in
M
with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, and in this case,
M
contains only the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, as in
eq.

15
. If there exists an
x\in X
such that
x\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
then other than the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M
also contains a v-structure
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
. In this case,
M
obeys
eq.

16
.

Suppose
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
.
Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
, we can partition the set of neighbors of
r_{2}
in
G_{2}
into
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
such that
Y=\{y:y-r_{2}\in M_{2}\}
is a set of
j
nodes, and
Y^{\prime}=\{y^{\prime}:r_{2}\rightarrow y^{\prime}\in M_{2}\}
of
\delta_{2}-j
nodes. From
lemma

2.7
, for
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
,
r_{2}\rightarrow y^{\prime}\in M
. Since
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, for
y\in Y
, either
r_{2}\rightarrow y\in M
or
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, otherwise,
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}-y
is an induced subgraph in
M
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.
If for two distinct
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
,
y_{1}\rightarrow r_{2},y_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
, then we get a v-structure
y_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y_{2}
in
M
such the nodes of the v-structure are in
V_{G_{2}}
, but the v-structure is not in
M_{2}
, implying
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])\neq\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
, which further implies
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{2}})\neq M_{2}
, a contradiction. This implies that there exists at most one
y\in Y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
. If there is no
y\in Y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
then there won’t be any v-structure in
M
with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, and in this case,
M
contains only the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, as in
eq.

15
. If there exists a
y\in Y
such that
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
then other than the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M
also contains a v-structure
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
. In this case,
M
obeys
eq.

17
.

This implies in all the possibilities,
M
either follows
eq.

15
or it follows
eq.

16
or it follows
eq.

17
. This completes the proof of
item

1
.
∎

As discussed in the introduction, there cannot be two distinct MECs with the same skeleton and the same set of v-structures. Uniqueness of the MECs present in
items

2
,
3
and
4
of
lemma

5.9
comes from this fact.
We now prove
items

2
,
3
and
4
of
lemma

5.9
.

Proof of
item

2
of
lemma

5.9
.

We construct an MEC of
G
that obeys
item

2
of
lemma

5.9
.
Let
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}-r_{2}\}
. To prove that
M
obeys
item

2
, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

15
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i+1)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC of
G
. From the construction of
M
, it is clear that the skeleton of
M
is
G
. Thus, the only thing we have to show is that
M
is an MEC, i.e., it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We start with proving
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, there cannot be an edge of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M_{1}
. Similarly, since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
, there cannot be an edge of the form
y\rightarrow r_{2}\in M_{2}
. Since
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}-r_{2}\}
, there cannot be an edge of the form
x\rightarrow r_{1}
and
y\rightarrow r_{2}
in
M
. This implies either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. If
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
then from the construction of
M
,
a\rightarrow b-c\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
T_{1}
, this contradicts
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. This implies that this case cannot occur. Similarly, there cannot be
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. This implies
M
cannot have any induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
.

We now show that each directed edge
u\rightarrow v
of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Let
u\rightarrow v
be a directed edge in
M
. From the construction of
M
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
then from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
or
w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
. From the construction of
M
, the induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Thus, when
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
,
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
for
M
.
Similarly, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
for
M
. This shows that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion implies that
M
obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.
This further implies that
M
is an MEC.

We now show that the v-structures of
M
obey
eq.

15
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are the induced subgraphs of
M
on
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Since
r_{1}-r_{2}\in M
, other than the v-structures of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M
cannot have any v-structures. This shows that the v-structures in
M
obey
eq.

15
.

We now show that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. From the construction of
M
,
M[V_{G_{1}}]=M_{1}
, and
M[V_{G_{2}}]=M_{2}
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
, and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. From
definition

2.5
, this further implies
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
.
In
M_{1}
,
i
edges adjacent to
r_{1}
are undirected and the remaining
\delta_{1}-i
adjacent edges of
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
are directed outward of
r_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
, the
i
adjacent undirected edges of
M_{1}
are undirected in
M
as well. Other than that, it has an additional undirected edge
r_{1}-r_{2}
, adjacent to
r_{1}
. This implies the total number of undirected edges adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M
is
i+1
. Therefore, no edge adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M
is incoming to
r_{1}
, and
i+1
undirected edges adjacent to
M
are undirected. This implies
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i+1)
. This completes the proof of
item

2
of
lemma

5.9
.

∎

Proof of
item

3
of
lemma

5.9
.

We construct an MEC of
G
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
.

2.

Pick an edge
x-r_{1}\in M_{1}
. Replace the edge
x-r_{1}
in
M
with
x\rightarrow r_{1}
.

3.

Update
M
by replacing
u-v
of
M
with
u\rightarrow v
, if
u-v\in M_{1}
and there exists an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
such that the path does not contain
x
.

We show that
M
constructed using the above steps will obey
item

3
of
lemma

5.9
. To prove this, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

16
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC i.e., it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We start with showing that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are three possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
.
One by one, we show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
,
b-c\in M_{1}
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change a directed edge in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\}
into an undirected edge), and either
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
or
a-b\in M_{1}
. If
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph in the MEC
M_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. And, if
a-b\in M_{1}
then it must have been directed at either at
item

2
(first case) or at
item

3
(second case). If it has been directed at
item

2
then
a=x,b=r_{1}
and
c\neq x
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
. This implies
r_{1}-c
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},c)
. But, then at
item

3
,
r_{1}-c
has been converted into
r_{1}\rightarrow c
. This implies that the first case cannot occur. We now move to the second case. Suppose
a-b
has been directed at
item

3
. This implies there exists a path from
r_{1}
to
(a,b)
in
M_{1}
. This further implies that there exists a path from
r_{1}
to
(b,c)
in
M_{1}
. But, then, we have
b\rightarrow c\in M
at
item

3
.
This implies that there cannot be an induced subgraph
a\rightarrow b-c
in
M
with
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M
with vertices in
V_{G_{2}}
is also an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
(note that
items

2
and
3
does not make any change in an edge with the endpoints in
V_{G_{2}}
). Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v-w\in M_{2}
. This shows that there cannot be an induced subgraph
a\rightarrow b-c
in
M
with
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
.

Suppose
a=r_{2}
,
b=r_{1}
, and
c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, this implies
r_{1}-c\in M_{1}
. There are two cases, either
c=x
, or
c\neq x
. If
c=x
, then at
item

2
, we have
c\rightarrow b\in M
. Therefore, this case cannot occur. Suppose
c\neq x
. But, edge
r_{1}-c
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},c)
. Then, it must have been directed at
item

3
. This implies that this possibility also cannot occur. This shows that
M
cannot have an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow v-w
. This implies that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
then either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u=r_{2}
and
v=r_{1}
. We show that in each of the possibilities
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, a directed edge of
M_{1}
is a directed edge in
M
. Therefore, since
u\rightarrow v\in M
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
or
u-v\in M_{1}
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
.
Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, a directed induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

When
u-v\in M_{1}
then it must have been directed in
M
either at
item

2
or at
item

3
. Suppose
u-v\in M_{1}
and it gets directed in
M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=x
and
v=r_{2}
. In that case,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
, obeying
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u-v\in M_{1}
and it gets directed in
M
at
item

3
. This implies there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
, and the path does not contain
x
.
Let the path be
P=(x_{0}=r_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1}=u,x_{l}=v)
for some
l\geq 1
. From the construction of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i+1}\in M
because
P_{i}=(x_{0}=r_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1})
is an undirected path in
M_{1}
from
r_{1}
to
(x_{i},x_{i+1})
, and
P_{i}
does not contain
x
either. If
l>1
then
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x_{l-2}\rightarrow x_{l-1}\rightarrow x_{l}
in
M
.
And, if
l=1
then
r_{1}=u
, and
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\rightarrow v
in
M
.
Thus, we show that if
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
in
M
. Thus, we show that each edge of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion shows that
M
is an MEC as it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. From the construction of
M
, the skelton of
M
is
G
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
.

We now show that
M
obeys
eq.

16
. From the construction of
M
, directed edges of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are directed edges of
M
. Also,
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x
is a v-structure in
M
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. For the completeness of the proof, we show that if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then either it is a v-structure of
M_{1}
or it is a v-structure of
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
and
w=x
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x\}
. This further implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}\leftarrow x\}
, i.e.,
M
obeys
eq.

16
.

Suppose there exists a v-structure
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
. From the construction of
M
, there are following possibilities: (a)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
in
V_{G_{1}}
, or (c)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
.

Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{1}
then
a\rightarrow b\in M
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{1}
or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, and either
v-w\in M_{1}
or
v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
. From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
or
u-v-w\in M_{1}
(recall that
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
).
If
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
then the v-structure is a v-structure of
M_{1}
. Suppose
u-v-w\in M_{1}
. Then, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it must have been directed either at
item

2
or at
item

2
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=x
and
v=r_{1}
. But, then
v-w
is a path from
r_{1}
to
(v,w)
(and the path does not contain
x
). And, we have
v\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
, a contradiction, as the v-structure implies
w\rightarrow v\in M
. This implies that this case cannot occur. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M
at
item

3
. Then, there exists a path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
, and the path does not contain
x
.
Since
u-v-w\in M_{1}
, the existence of a path from
r_{1}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{1}
implies the existence of a path from
r_{1}
to
(v,w)
in
M_{1}
, and the new path also does not contain
x
. Also, since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, there cannot be two different paths from
r_{1}
to
w
.
This implies at
item

2
, if we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
then we also have
v\rightarrow w\in M
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have assumed that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.
This implies there cannot be
u-v-w\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.

Suppose
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{1}
in
V_{G_{1}}
. Since
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0)
, either
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M_{1}
or
r_{1}-w\in M_{1}
. From the construction of
M
, if
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M_{1}
then
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M
. But, we have
r_{1}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies that
r_{1}-w\in M_{1}
. From
items

2
and
3
, if we have
w\rightarrow r_{1}\in M
then
w=x
, otherwise, we have
r_{1}\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
as
r_{1}-w
is an undirected path from
r_{1}
to
(r_{1},w)
, and the path does not contain
x
. This implies
u=r_{2}
,
v=r_{1}
, and
w=x
.

We now move to the final possibility. Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies that if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{2}
.
Thus, we show that in all the possible scenarios, if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then it is a v-structure either in
M_{2}
or in
M_{1}
, or
v=r_{1}
and
\{u,w\}=\{r_{2},x\}
.
This completes the proof that
M
obeys
eq.

16
.

The above discussion implies that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Therefore, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Since there is an incoming edge
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
in
M
,
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This completes the proof of
item

3
of
lemma

5.9
.
∎

Proof of
item

4
of
lemma

5.9
.

We construct an MEC of
G
using the following steps:

1.

Initialize
M=M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
.

2.

Pick an edge
y-r_{2}\in M_{2}
. Replace the edge
y-r_{2}
in
M
with
y\rightarrow r_{2}
.

3.

Update
M
by replacing
u-v
of
M
with
u\rightarrow v
, if
u-v\in M_{2}
and there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
such that the path does not contain
y
.

We show that
M
constructed using the above steps will obey
item

4
of
lemma

5.9
. To prove this, we have to show the following: (a)
M
is an MEC of
G
, (b)
M
obeys
eq.

17
, (c)
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, and (d)
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
.

We first show that
M
is an MEC i.e., it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We start with proving that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
M
contains an induced subgraph of the form
a\rightarrow b-c
. From the construction of
M
, there are three possibilities: either (a)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
.
One by one, we show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
. From the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M
with vertices in
V_{G_{1}}
is also an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
(note that
items

2
and
3
does not make any change in an edge with the endpoints in
V_{G_{1}}
). Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, there cannot be an induced subgraph
a\rightarrow b-c\in M_{1}
. This implies that the first possibility cannot occur.

Suppose
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
,
b-c\in M_{2}
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change a directed edge in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\}
into an undirected edge), and either
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
or
a-b\in M_{2}
. If
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
then
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph in the MEC
M_{2}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
. And, if
a-b\in M_{2}
then it must have been directed at either at
item

2
(first case) or at
item

3
(second case). If it has directed at
item

2
then
a=y,b=r_{2}
and
c\neq y
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
. This implies
r_{2}-c
is a path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},c)
. But, then at
item

3
,
r_{2}-c
has been converted into
r_{2}\rightarrow c
. This implies that the first case cannot occur. We move to the second case when
a-b
has been directed at
item

3
. This implies there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(a,b)
in
M_{2}
. This further implies that there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(b,c)
in
M_{2}
. But, then, we have
b\rightarrow c\in M
at
item

3
. This shows that the second case also cannot occur. This further implies the second possibility also cannot occur. We now move to the third possibility.

Suppose
a=r_{1}
,
b=r_{2}
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, this implies
r_{2}-c\in M_{2}
. There are two cases, either
c=y
, or
c\neq y
. If
c=y
, then at
item

2
, we have
c\rightarrow b\in M
. Therefore, this case cannot occur. Suppose
c\neq y
. But, edge
r_{2}-c
is a path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},c)
. Then, it must have been directed at
item

3
. This implies that the third possibility also cannot occur. This shows that
M
cannot have an induced subgraph of the form
u\rightarrow v-w
. This implies that
M
obeys
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
then either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u=r_{1}
and
v=r_{2}
. We show that in each of the possibilities
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, then from the construction of
M
,
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{1}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. Since from the construction of
M
, an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
or
u-v\in M_{2}
. Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
. Since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, from
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph of
M_{2}
of the form either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
or
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
. From the construction of
M
, a directed induced subgraph of
M_{2}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(as a directed edge of
M_{2}
is a directed edge of
M
, and the skeletons of
M_{2}
and
M[V_{G_{2}}]
are the same). Therefore, in this case also
u\rightarrow v
in
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

Suppose
u-v\in M_{2}
. Then, it must have been directed in
M
either at
item

2
or at
item

3
. Suppose
u-v\in M_{2}
and it gets directed in
M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=y
and
v=r_{2}
. In that case,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
, obeying
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
. Suppose
u-v
gets directed in
M
at
item

3
. This implies there exists an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
, and the path does not contain
y
.
Let the path be
P=(x_{0}=r_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1}=u,x_{l}=v)
for some
l\geq 1
. From the construction of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i+1}\in M
because
P_{i}=(x_{0}=r_{2},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1})
is an undirected path in
M_{2}
from
r_{2}
to
(x_{i},x_{i+1})
, and
P_{i}
does not contain
y
either. If
l>1
then
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x_{l-2}\rightarrow x_{l-1}\rightarrow x_{l}
in
M
.
And, if
l=1
then
r_{2}=u
, and
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\rightarrow v
in
M
.
Thus, we show that if
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

We now show that
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
in
M
also obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
is part of an induced subgraph
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
in
M
. Thus, we show that each edge of
M
obeys
item

2
of
theorem

3.1
.

The above discussion shows that
M
is an MEC as it obeys
items

1
and
2
of
theorem

3.1
. From the construction of
M
, the skelton of
M
is
G
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
.

We now show that
M
obeys
eq.

17
. From the construction of
M
, directed edges of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are directed edges of
M
. Also,
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y
is a v-structure in
M
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
. For the completeness of the proof, we show that if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then either it is a v-structure of
M_{1}
or it is a v-structure of
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{1},v=r_{2}
and
w=y
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}
. This further implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\{r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}\leftarrow y\}
, i.e.,
M
obeys
eq.

17
.

Suppose there exists a v-structure
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
. From the construction of
M
, there are following possibilities: (a)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
, or (b)
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
in
V_{G_{2}}
, or (c)
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
.
From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
(note that
items

2
and
3
do not change the orientation of any edge of
M_{1}
). This implies that if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{1}
.

Suppose
u=r_{1}
,
v=r_{2}
, and
w
is a neighbor of
r_{2}
in
V_{G_{2}}
. Since
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0)
, either
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
or
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
. From the construction of
M
(
items

1
,
2
and
3
), if
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M_{2}
then
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M
. But, we have
r_{2}\leftarrow w\in M
. This implies that
r_{2}-w\in M_{2}
. From
items

2
and
3
, if we have
w\rightarrow r_{2}\in M
then
w=y
, otherwise, we have
r_{2}\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
as
r_{2}-w
is an undirected path from
r_{2}
to
(r_{2},w)
, and the path does not contain
y
. This implies
u=r_{1},v=r_{2}
, and
w=y
.

We now move to the final possibility.
Suppose
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
. From the construction of
M
, if
a\rightarrow b\in M_{2}
then
a\rightarrow b\in M
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{2}
or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
, and either
v-w\in M_{2}
or
v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
. From
item

1
of
theorem

3.1
, either
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
or
u-v-w\in M_{2}
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M_{2}
. Then, the v-structure is a v-structure of
M_{2}
.

Suppose
u-v-w\in M_{2}
. Then, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it must have been directed either at
item

2
or at
item

2
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at
item

2
. Then,
u=y
and
v=r_{2}
. But, then
v-w
is a path from
(r_{2})
to
(v,w)
(and the path does not contain
y
). And, we have
v\rightarrow w\in M
at
item

3
, a contradiction, as the v-structure implies
w\rightarrow v\in M
. This implies that this case cannot occur.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at
item

3
. Then, there exists a path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
, and the path does not contain
y
.
Since
u-v-w\in M_{2}
, the existence of a path from
r_{2}
to
(u,v)
in
M_{2}
implies the existence of a path from
r_{2}
to
(v,w)
in
M_{2}
, and the new path also does not contain
y
. Also, since
M_{2}
is an MEC of a tree graph
G_{2}
, there cannot be two different paths from
r_{2}
to
w
.
This implies at
item

3
, if we have
u\rightarrow v\in M
then we also have
v\rightarrow w\in M
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have assumed that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
.
This implies there cannot be
u-v-w\in M_{2}
and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\in M
. Thus, we show that in all the possible scenarios, if
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
then it is a v-structure either in
M_{1}
or in
M_{2}
, or
u=r_{1},v=r_{2}
and
w=y
.
This completes the proof that
M
obeys
eq.

17
.

The above discussion implies that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}]=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{2}}]=\mathcal{V}(M_{2})
. Therefore, from
definition

2.5
,
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

From the construction of
M
,
M_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M
.
In
M_{1}
,
i
edges adjacent to
r_{1}
are undirected and the remaining
\delta_{1}-i
adjacent edges of
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
are directed outward of
r_{1}
. The only edge that is adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M
but not adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M_{1}
is
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{2}
, which is directed outward of
r_{1}
. Therefore, no edge adjacent to
r_{1}
in
M
is incoming to
r_{1}
, and
i
undirected edges adjacent to
M
are undirected. This implies
M\in\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
.

This completes the proof of
item

4
of
lemma

5.9
.
∎

This completes the proof of
lemma

5.9
.
∎

Lemma

5.7
implies the following corollary:

Corollary 5.10
.

For
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
,
the number of MECs
M
of
G
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
is
i+j+1
. More specifically,
i
MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
j
MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
, and one MEC belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i+1)
.

Proof.

From
item

1
of
lemma

5.9
, for any MEC
M
of
G
, if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
then
M
has to obey either
eqs.

15
,
16
and
17
.
From
item

2
of
lemma

5.9
, there exists a unique MEC that satisfies
eq.

15
, belonging to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i+1)
.

Given
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
,
i
instances of
x
exist such that
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
. According to
item

3
of
lemma

5.9
, for each
x-r_{1}\in E_{M_{1}}
, there exists a unique MEC satisfying
eq.

16
. Consequently, there are
i
MECs following
eq.

16
, all belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
as per
item

3
.

Given
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
,
j
instances of
y
exist such that
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
. According to
item

4
of
lemma

5.9
, for each
y-r_{2}\in E_{M_{2}}
, there exists a unique MEC satisfying
eq.

17
. Consequently, there are
j
MECs following
eq.

17
, all belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
as per
item

4
.

Hence, the total count of MECs is
i+j+1
. Out of which,
i
MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
, and
j
MECs belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
, and one MEC belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i+1)
.
∎

From
corollary

5.4
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there are two MECs of
G
that belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. From
corollary

5.6
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
, there are
j+2
MECs of
G
that belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
.
From
corollary

5.8
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there are
i+1
MECs of
G
that belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. From
corollary

5.10
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
, there are
i
MECs of
G
that belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
. This makes the total number of MECs of
G
that belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},1)
equal to as in
item

1
of
lemma

3.2
.

From
corollaries

5.4
and
5.6
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2})
, no MEC of
G
belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0)
.
From
corollary

5.8
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},1)
, there are one MEC of
G
that belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
. From
corollary

5.10
, corresponding to each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0,j)
, there are
j
MECs of
G
that belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
. From
corollary

5.10
, for each tuple
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},r_{1},0,i-1)
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},r_{2},0)
, there are one MEC of
G
that belong to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
. This makes total number of MECs of
G
that belongs to
\text{MEC}(G,r_{1},0,i)
equal to as in
items

2
and
3
of
lemma

3.2
.

This completes the proof of
lemma

3.2
.

5.2
Omitted proof of
section

3.2

We first go through some results of
Sharma 2023
:

Definition 5.11

(Triangle Free Path,
Sharma 2023
)
.

For a graph
G
, a path
P=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{l})
is said to be a
triangle-free path
of
G
if for any
1\leq i\leq l-2
, there does not exist an edge between
u_{i}
and
u_{i+2}
in
G
, i.e., neither
u_{i}-u_{i+2}\in E_{G}
nor
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{i+2}\in E_{G}
nor
u_{i}\leftarrow u_{i+2}\in E_{G}
.

Lemma 5.12

(
Sharma 2023
)
.

For an undirected chordal graph
G
, if
P
is a
triangle-free path
of
G
then
P
is a chordless path of
G
.

Observation 5.13

(Observation 2.30 of
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
M
be an MEC, and
P=(u_{1}=x,u_{2}=y,\ldots,u_{l-1}=u,u_{l}=v)
be a
triangle-free path
from
(x,y)
to
(u,v)
in
M
. If
u-v\in M
then
P
is an undirected chordless path in
M
, more specifically
x-y\in M
.

Lemma 5.14

(Corollary 2.50 of
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
M
be an MEC and
M^{\prime}
be a projection of
M
.
Let
P=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{l})
be a
triangle-free path
in
M^{\prime}
. If
u_{2}\rightarrow u_{1}\notin M
then
P
is also a
triangle-free path
in
M
.

Lemma 5.15

(Corollary 2.51 of
Sharma 2023
)
.

Let
M
be an MEC and
M^{\prime}
be a projection of
M
.
If
P=(u_{1}=x,u_{2}=y,u_{3},\ldots,u_{l-1}=u,u_{l}=v)
is a
triangle-free path
from
(x,y)
to
(u,v)
in
M^{\prime}
, and
x\rightarrow y\in M
then for all
1\leq i<l
,
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{i+1}\in M
.

Proof of
item

3
of
3.6
.

W.l.o.g., let us assume
i=1
.
We start with the following observation.

Observation 5.16
.

Let
P=(u_{0}=x,u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}=y)
be a chordless path in
G_{1}
from
x
to
y
such that
l\geq 2
, and
x,y\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. Then, each node of
P
is in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.

Proof.

There are two possibilities: either
x\in r_{1}
, or
x\notin r_{1}
.

1.

Suppose
x\in r_{1}
. Then, we claim that
y\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
.

Suppose
y\in r_{1}
. Then, since
r_{1}
is a clique of
G_{1}
,
x-y\in G_{1}
. But, this implies
P
is not a chordless path in
G_{1}
, a contradiction. This implies
y\notin r_{1}
.
This further implies
y\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
.

Since
y\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
, there must exist a node
z\in r_{1}
such that
y\in N(z,G_{1})
, i.e.,
z-y\in G_{1}
. Since
r_{1}
is a clique in
G_{1}
, and
x,z\in r_{1}
,
x-z\in G_{1}
. This implies
u_{0}-z,u_{l}-z\in G_{1}
.
We claim that each node in
P
is a neighbor of
z
in
G_{1}
.

Suppose there exists a node
u_{i}
which is not a neighbor of
z
. Pick the least such
i
. Since
u_{0}-z\in G_{1}
,
i>0
. Pick the least
j
such that
j>i
and
u_{j}-z\in G_{1}
(there must exist such
j
as
u_{l}-z\in G_{1}
). Then
(z,u_{i-1},u_{i},\ldots,u_{j},z)
is a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
(since
P
is a chordless path, there cannot be an edge between two non-adjacent nodes
u_{a}
and
u_{b}
of
P
), a contradiction, as
G_{1}
is a chordal graph. This implies each node in
P
is a neighbor of
z
in
G_{1}
. Since
z\in r_{1}
, this further implies each node of
P
is in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.

2.

Suppose
x\notin r_{1}
. Since
x\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
and
x\notin r_{1}
,
x\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
.
Then, there must exist a node
z\in r_{1}
such that
x\in N(z,G_{1})
, i.e.,
u_{0}-z\in G_{1}
. There are two possibilities: either
z-y\in G_{1}
or
z-y\notin G_{1}
.

Suppose
z-y\in G_{1}
. This implies
u_{0}-z\in G_{1}
and
u_{l}-z\in G_{1}
. Then, similar to the previous case, each node of the chordless path
P
is in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.

Suppose
z-y\notin G_{1}
. This implies
y\notin r_{1}
. Because, if
y\in r_{1}
then since
r_{1}
is a clique of
G_{1}
, we have
z-y\in G_{1}
, contradicting our assumption. Since
y\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
and
y\notin r_{1}
,
y\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
. Then, there must exist a node
z^{\prime}\in r_{1}
such that
y\in N(z^{\prime},G_{1})
. We show that each node of the chordless path
P
is either a neighbor of
z
or a neighbor of
z^{\prime}
.

Suppose there exists a node
u_{i}
in
P
which is not a neighbor of
z
in
G_{1}
, i.e.,
u_{i}-z\notin G_{1}
. Pick the least such
i
.
i>0
as
u_{0}-z\in G_{1}
. This implies
u_{i-1}-z\in G_{1}
. We claim that for any
j\geq i
,
z-u_{j}\notin G_{1}
. Suppose there exists a
j>i
such that
z-u_{j}\in G_{1}
(
j\neq i
as we have assumed
u_{i}-z\notin G_{1}
). Pick the least
j
. Then
(z,u_{i-1},u_{i},\ldots,u_{j},z)
is a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
(there cannot be an edge between two non-adjacent nodes
u_{a}
and
u_{b}
of
P
, as
P
is a chordless path), a contradiction, as
G_{1}
is a chordal graph. We further claim that for each
j\geq i-1
,
z^{\prime}-u_{j}\in G_{1}
.

Suppose there exists
j\geq i-1
such that
z^{\prime}-u_{j}\notin G_{1}
. Pick the least such
j
. Now, pick the least
k>j
such that
z^{\prime}-u_{k}\in G_{1}
(there must exist such
k
because
u_{l}-z^{\prime}\in G_{1}
). If
j=i-1
then
C=(z^{\prime},z,u_{i-1},u_{i},\ldots,u_{k},z^{\prime})
is a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
, and if
j>i-1
then
C=(z^{\prime},u_{j-1},u_{j},\ldots,u_{k},z^{\prime})
is a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
. Both give a contradiction, as
G_{1}
is a chordal graph. This implies that for each
j\geq i-1
,
z^{\prime}-u_{j}\in G_{1}
.

This further implies each node of
P
is either a neighbor of
z
or a neighbor of
z^{\prime}
. Since both
z
and
z^{\prime}
are in
r_{1}
, nodes of
P
are in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.

Therefore, in all the possibilities, nodes of
P
are in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. This completes the proof of
5.16
.
∎

From
item

3
of
lemma

3.7
, for
u-v\in O_{1}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in O
then either
(a)
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in

(

3a
of

3.7
), or

1.

, i.e., there exists

.

. From

From

, all the nodes of
P
is in

. Since

is an induced subgraph of

, and

.
Since

, and

to

, from

. This further implies

, and

.a (recall that

).

2.

Suppose
u-v\in O_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\in O
because

obeys

3c
of

3.7
, i.e., there exists

, and there exist

, and

with

. Since

and

,

.
Both

, otherwise, concatenation of

. This implies nodes of

, as

.
Since the skeleton of

Also, since

. From

, all the nodes of

and

. Since

, and

,

and

are chordless paths in
O_{1}
.

We claim that
u-a_{l-1}\in O_{1}
. We first show that
u-a_{l-1}\in G_{1}
.
Suppose
u-a_{l-1}\notin G_{1}
, Then, we claim that for all
i\leq l-1
,
u-a_{i}\notin G_{1}
.
Suppose for some
j<l-1
,
u-a_{j}\in G_{1}
. Pick the highest such
j
. Then,
C=(u,a_{j},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{l-1},a_{l}=v,u)
is a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
, a contradiction, as
