is a chordal graph. Then, we claim that
C=(b_{m}=a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}=b_{0},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
is a chordless cycle. Suppose
C
is not a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
. Then, since
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is a chordless path in
G_{1}
, there must exist
i
and
j
such that
0<i<l
,
0<j<m
and
a_{i}-b_{j}\in G_{1}
. Pick the highest such
i
for which there exists a
j
such that
0<j<m
and
a_{i}-b_{j}\in G_{1}
. Then, pick the smallest such
j
. As shown earlier, for all
i\leq l-1
,
u-a_{i}\notin G_{1}
. Therefore
j>1
(remember
u=b_{1}
). But, then
C=(a_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{l}=b_{0},b_{1},\ldots,b_{j},a_{i})
is a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
. In any way, we get a chordless cycle in
G_{1}
, a contradiction, as
G_{1}
is a chordal graph. This implies
u-a_{l-1}\in G_{1}
.

Since
u,a_{l-1}
are in the same undirected connected component of
M_{1}
(an MEC of
G_{1}
), and
u-a_{l-1}\in G_{1}
(the skeleton of
M_{1}
),
u-a_{l-1}\in M_{1}
(edge between two nodes of the same undirected connected component of an MEC must be undirected, otherwise, it will create a directed cycle and violate
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
). Since
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
, and
u,a_{l-1}\in V_{O_{1}}
(as shown earlier),
u-a_{l-1}\in O_{1}
.

We now prove that
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
.
We first show that for each
0\leq i<l
,
a_{i}\rightarrow a_{i+1}\in O
.
Since
O
is the projection of
M
, and
u\rightarrow v,x\rightarrow y\in O
,
u\rightarrow v,x\rightarrow y\in M
.
Since
M_{1}
is a projection of
M
, and
P_{1}
is a
triangle-free path
in
M_{1}
from
(x,y)
to
v
such that
x\rightarrow y\in M
, from
lemma

5.15
, for
0\leq i<l
,
a_{i}\rightarrow a_{i+1}\in M
.
Since nodes of
P_{1}
belong to
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})\subseteq V_{O}
, and
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
, for
0\leq i<l
,
a_{i}\rightarrow a_{i+1}\in O
.

Since from the construction, the skeletons of
O[V_{O_{1}}]
and
O_{1}
are the same, either
u\rightarrow a_{l-1}\in O
, or
u-a_{l-1}\in O
, or
u\leftarrow a_{l-1}\in O
. We show that in each possibility,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
.
If
u\rightarrow a_{l-1}\in O
then
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
as it is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow a_{l-1}\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
, as shown in
fig.

1
.c. We now show that in other possibilities also
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected.

Suppose either
u-a_{l-1}\in O
or
u\leftarrow a_{l-1}\in O
.
We claim that
b_{2}\rightarrow b_{1}\in O
.
Suppose
b_{2}\rightarrow b_{1}\notin O
. Since
P_{2}
is a
triangle-free path
in
M_{1}
, the subpath
P_{2}^{\prime}=(b_{2}=u,b_{3},\ldots,b_{m})
of
P
is a
triangle-free path
in
M_{1}
.
If
b_{2}\rightarrow b_{1}\notin M
then from
lemma

5.14
,
P_{2}^{\prime}
is a
triangle-free path
in
M
. As discussed earlier, each edge of the
triangle-free path

P_{1}
is directed in
O
. This implies a subpath of
P
,
P_{1}^{\prime}=(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{l-1})
, is a directed
triangle-free path
in
O
. Also, from our assumption, either
a_{l-1}-b_{2}\in O
or
a_{l-1}\rightarrow b_{2}\in O
.
Since
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
, concatenaiton of
P_{1}^{\prime}
,
a_{l-1}-b_{2}
(or
a_{l-1}\rightarrow b_{2}
), and
P_{2}^{\prime}
makes a directed cycle in
M
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
. This implies if
u-a_{l-1}\in O
or
u\leftarrow a_{l-1}\in O
then
b_{2}\rightarrow b_{1}\in O
. But, then,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
due to part of an induced subgraph of the form
b_{2}\rightarrow(b_{1}=u)\rightarrow(b_{0}=v)
.

Thus, we show that for chordal graph
G
,
items

3b
and
3c
of
lemma

3.7
imply
item

3a
of
lemma

3.7
.
This completes the proof
item

3
of
3.6
.
∎

Proof of
3.15
.

We start with the following claim and observations:

Claim 5.17
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, for any three nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
, there does not exist a directed cycle
(u,v,w,u)
in
M
.

Observation 5.18
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{i}
then
u\rightarrow v\in M
.

Proof.

W.l.o.g., let us assume
a=1
.
Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
.
From
definition

2.13
, at step
1
of the construction of
M
,
u\rightarrow v\in M
. At step
2
, we replace an undirected edge with a directed one. This implies once an edge is directed in
M
at step
1
, it remains directed in
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow v\in M
. This completes the proof of
5.18
.
∎

Corollary 5.19
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
,
u,v\in V_{G_{i}}
, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then either
u-v\in M_{i}
or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{i}
.

Proof.

W.l.o.g., let us assume
a=1
.
Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
and
u\rightarrow v\in M
.
From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{G_{1}}]})=\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
v\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
. But, if
v\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
then from
5.18
,
v\rightarrow u\in M
, a contradiction, as
u\rightarrow v\in M
. Thus, the options that remain are
u-v\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
. This completes the proof of
corollary

5.19
.
∎

Corollary 5.20
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, for
u,v\in V_{G_{i}}
, if
u-v\in M
then
u-v\in M_{i}
.

Proof.

W.l.o.g., let us assume
i=1
.
Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
and
u-v\in M
.
From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{G_{1}}]})=\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})
. This implies either
u-v\in M_{1}
, or
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, or
v\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
. But, if
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
or
v\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
then from
5.18
,
u\rightarrow v\in M
or
v\rightarrow u\in M
, a contradiction, as
u-v\in M
. Thus, the option that remains is
u-v\in M_{1}
.
This completes the proof of
corollary

5.20
.
∎

The above claim, observation, and corollaries imply the following:

Observation 5.21
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, for any three nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to
V_{G_{i}}
, there does not exist a directed cycle
(u,v,w,u)
in
M
.

Proof.

Contrary to
5.21
, suppose for some
i\in\{1,2\}
, there exist nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to
V_{G_{i}}
such that
C=(u,v,w,u)
is a directed cycle in
M
. From
corollaries

5.19
and
5.20
,
C
is a cycle in
M_{i}
. Since
M_{i}
is an MEC, from
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
, none of the edges of the cycle is directed in
M_{i}
. This implies all the node belongs to the same undirected connected component of
M_{i}
. This contradicts
5.17
. This implies our assumption is wrong. This proves
5.21
.
∎

5.21
implies the following:

Observation 5.22
.

There do not exist three nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
in
M
such that
(u,v,w,u)
forms a directed cycle in
M
.

Proof.

Contrary to
5.22
, let us assume that there exist
u
,
v
, and
w
in
M
such that
(u,v,w,u)
forms a directed cycle in
M
.
We claim that either all the nodes of the cycle belong to
V_{G_{1}}
(i.e.,
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
) or all the nodes belong to
V_{G_{2}}
(i.e.,
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
).

Suppose neither all the nodes of the cycle are in
V_{G_{1}}
nor all the nodes are in
V_{G_{2}}
. This implies there exists a node in the cycle that belongs to
V_{G_{1}}\setminus V_{G_{2}}
and another node in the cycle that belongs to
V_{G_{2}}\setminus V_{G_{1}}
. However, from the construction, there are no edges between
V_{G_{1}}\setminus V_{G_{2}}
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus V_{G_{1}}
in
G
because
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
in
G
. According to
3.12
,
G
is the skeleton of
M
. This implies there cannot be an edge between nodes in
V_{G_{1}}\setminus V_{G_{2}}
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus V_{G_{1}}
. But, in
M
, there exists an edge between each pair of nodes in the cycle. This implies that the scenario where a node of the cycle is in
V_{G_{1}}\setminus V_{G_{2}}
and another in
V_{G_{2}}\setminus V_{G_{1}}
cannot occur. Therefore, all the nodes of the cycle must either be in
V_{G_{1}}
or in
V_{G_{2}}
.

From
5.21
, there cannot exist a directed cycle
(u,v,w,u)
in
M
such that all the nodes are either in
V_{G_{1}}
or
V_{G_{2}}
. Hence, our assumption is wrong. This completes the proof of
5.22
.
∎

We now demonstrate that
5.22
implies the absence of any directed cycle in
M
. Let’s assume the existence of a directed cycle in
M
. Select the smallest-length directed cycle
C=(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}=u_{0})
in
M
. Our claim is that
l=2
.

Suppose
l>2
. As
G
is the skeleton of
M
,
C=(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{l},u_{l+1}=u_{0})
forms a cycle in
G
. Since
G
is chordal, an edge in
G
must exist between two non-adjacent nodes
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
of
C
. Without loss of generality, let’s assume
i<j
. Then, either
u_{i}-u_{j}\in M
or
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{j}\in M
or
u_{j}\rightarrow u_{i}\in M
. Consequently, either
(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{i},u_{j},u_{j+1},\ldots,u_{l},u_{l+1}=u_{0})
or
(u_{j},u_{i},u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{j})
forms a directed cycle in
M
with a smaller length than cycle
C
. This contradicts our choice of picking the smallest directed cycle. Thus,
l=2
.

From
5.22
, there cannot exist a directed cycle of length two in
M
. Therefore, no directed cycle exists in
M
, indicating that
M
is a chain graph. This completes the proof of
3.15
.
∎

Proof of
5.17
.

The following two claims prove
5.17
.

Claim 5.23
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, for any three nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}=\varnothing
, there does not exist a directed cycle
(u,v,w,u)
in
M
.

Claim 5.24
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, for any three nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}\neq\varnothing
, there does not exist a directed cycle
(u,v,w,u)
in
M
.

For any
i\in\{1,2\}
, an undirected connected component of
M_{i}
can be of two types: either
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}=\varnothing
or
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}\neq\varnothing
.
5.23
and
5.24
show that in both of the possibilities,
5.17
is valid. This completes the proof of
5.17
.
∎

Proof of
5.23
.

Contrary to
5.23
, let us assume that for some
i\in\{1,2\}
, there exists
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}=\varnothing
, and
(u,v,w,u)
is a directed cycle in
M
. From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{\mathcal{C}}]})=\mathcal{C}
. This implies
u-v,v-w,w-c\in\mathcal{C}
.
Before moving ahead, we go through the following claim.

Claim 5.25
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\mathcal{C}
be an undirected connected component of
M_{i}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}=\varnothing
. Then, for
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
,
u-v\in M
.

From
5.25
,
u-v,v-w,w-c\in M
. This implies
(u,v,w,u)
is an undirected cycle in
M
, contradicting our assumption. This implies that our assumption is wrong. This completes the proof of
5.23
.
∎

Proof of
5.25
.

Contrary to
5.25
, let us assume that for some
i\in\{1,2\}
, there exists an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}=\varnothing
, and there exists
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u\rightarrow v\in M
. W.l.o.g., let us assume
i=1
.

Before moving ahead, we go through the following observation:

Observation 5.26
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\mathcal{C}
be an undirected connected component of
M_{i}
, and
u-v
be an edge of
\mathcal{C}
such that
u\rightarrow v\in M
. Then,
u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
either at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.

From
5.26
, if
u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
then it must have been added either at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or at step
2
of the construction of
M
, when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.
Since
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}=\varnothing
,
\mathcal{C}
has not been considered at step
2
of the construction of
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
, each edge of
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected edge of
M_{1}
. This implies
u-v\in M_{1}
. Since
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
, the following observation implies
u\rightarrow v\in O
.

Observation 5.27
.

If
u-v\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
(i.e.,
u\rightarrow v
added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
) then
u\rightarrow v\in O
.

From
5.27
,
u\rightarrow v\in O
.
This further implies that for
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then
u\rightarrow v\in O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
, contradicting
5.28
.

Observation 5.28
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
such that
r_{1}\cap V_{\mathcal{C}}=\varnothing
. Then,
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
is an undirected graph.

This implies our assumption was wrong that
u\rightarrow v\in M
.
This completes the proof of

5.25
.
∎

Proof of
5.26
.

W.l.o.g., let us assume
i=1
.
If
u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
then we are done. Suppose
u\rightarrow v
has not been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
. Then, it must have added to
M
at step
2
when some undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
M_{1}
or
M_{2}
is considered. We show at step
2
of the construction of
M
, when any other undirected connected component is considered,
u\rightarrow v
has not been added to
M
.

Since two different undirected connected component of an MEC does not share any vertices, for any other undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
M_{1}
,
V_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\cap V_{\mathcal{C}}=\varnothing
. This implies
u-v\notin\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
u\rightarrow v
has not been added to
M
at step
2
when
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
has been considered.

For any undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
M_{2}
,
V_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\cap V_{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq r_{1}\cap r_{2}\subseteq r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G)
. This implies if
u-v\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
then
u,v\in r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
. But, at step
2
of the construction of
M
, when
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
has been considered, we can added
u\rightarrow v
to
M
only if
v\notin r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
. This implies
u\rightarrow v
has not been added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
, when
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
has been considered.

The above discussion implies that for
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
, if
u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
then it must have been added either at step
1
of the construction of
M
or at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.
∎

Proof of
5.27
.

From
definition

2.13
, if
u-v\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
then either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
or
u\rightarrow v\in O
.

u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
implies that
u,v\in V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{M_{2}}=I
.
Since
O_{2}=M_{2}[V_{O_{2}}]
, for
u,v\in I
,
u\rightarrow v\in M_{2}
implies
u\rightarrow v\in O_{2}
, as from the construction,
I\subseteq V_{O_{2}}
.
Since
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
, therefore, from
item

1
of
definition

3.8
, we have
u\rightarrow v\in O
.
Thus, we can say that if
u-v\in M_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
then
u\rightarrow v\in O
.
∎

Proof of
5.28
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
such that
r_{1}\cap V_{\mathcal{C}}=\varnothing
. Contrary to
5.28
, let us assume that
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
is not an undirected graph, i.e., there exists a directed edge
u\rightarrow v\in O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
.
Pick
u
and
v
using the following steps:

1.

Pick
u
such that there is no incoming edge to
u
in
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
.

2.

Pick
v
from the set
Y=\{y:u\rightarrow y\in O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]\}
such that for any
y\in Y
,
y\rightarrow v\notin O
.

The existence of such
u
and
v
is because
O
is a chain graph.
Since
u\rightarrow v\in O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
,
u\rightarrow v\in O
. From
item

3
of
definition

3.8
,
u\rightarrow v
must be strongly protected in
O
.
We now show that
u\rightarrow v
is not strongly protected in
O
. This leads to a contradiction, which further implies that
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
is an undirected graph.

We go through the following claims and observations to prove
u\rightarrow v
is not strongly protected in
O
.

Claim 5.29
.

For any
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
,
x\rightarrow u\notin O
.

Proof of
5.29
.

Contrary to
5.29
, suppose there exists an
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
x\rightarrow u\in O
. This implies
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}
, and
x\rightarrow u\in O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
, contradicting our choice of
u
(recall that from the choice of
u
(
item

1
), there is no incoming edge to
u
in
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
). This implies that our assumption is wrong. This completes the proof of
5.29
.
∎

Observation 5.30
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}=\varnothing
. Then, for any
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
,
N(x,G)\subseteq V_{G_{1}}
.

Proof of
5.30
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}=\varnothing
,
x
is a node in
V_{\mathcal{C}}
, and
y
is a neighbor of
x
in
G
.
We show that
y\in V_{G_{1}}
.

Since
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}=\varnothing
, and
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
,
x\in V_{G_{1}}\setminus r_{1}
.
Since
I=r_{1}\cap r_{2}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
,
x\in V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
. This implies
y\notin V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Since
V_{G}
is a disjoint union of
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
, this further implies
y\in V_{G_{1}}
. This implies
N(x,G)\subseteq V_{G_{1}}
. This completes the proof of
5.30
.
∎

Observation 5.31
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
. For
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
, if
x\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
then
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
.

Proof of
5.31
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
, there exists an undirected edge
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
, and there exists a directed edge
x\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
. There are two possibilities: either
x-v\in G_{1}
or
x-v\notin G_{1}
(remember
G_{1}
is the skeleton of
M_{1}
). If
x-v\notin G_{1}
then
x\rightarrow u-v
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
, contradicting
item

3
of
theorem

2.2
. This implies
x-v\in G_{1}
. Then, there must be
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
, otherwise,
(x,u,v,x)
is a directed cycle in
M
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
. This completes the proof of
5.31
.
∎

Claim 5.32
.

For any
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
,
x\rightarrow u\in O
if, and only if,
x\rightarrow v\in O
.

Proof of
5.32
.

From
5.30
, the neighbors of
\mathcal{C}
in
M
is in
V_{G_{1}}
. Since
u\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
,
v\in V_{G_{1}}
.
For any
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
, from
corollary

5.19
, if
x\rightarrow u\in M
then
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
(if
x-u\in M_{1}
then
x
and
u
are in the same undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{1}
). From
5.31
, for any
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
, if
x\rightarrow u\in M_{1}
then
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
.
From
5.18
, if
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
then
x\rightarrow v\in M
. This implies that for
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
, if
x\rightarrow u\in M
then
x\rightarrow v\in M
.
Since
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
, and
v\in V_{O}
, for
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
, if
x\rightarrow u\in O
then
x\rightarrow v\in O
.
Similarly, we can show that for
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
, if
x\rightarrow v\in O
then
x\rightarrow u\in O
. This implies that for
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}
,
x\rightarrow u\in O
if, and only if,
x\rightarrow v\in O
.
∎

Claim 5.33
.

u\rightarrow v
cannot be part of an induced subgraph
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
in
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.a).

Proof of
5.33
.

From
5.29
, if
w\in\mathcal{C}
then
w\rightarrow u\notin O
. And, from
5.32
, if
w\notin\mathcal{C}
and
w\rightarrow u\in O
then
w\rightarrow v\in O
. This implies
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
is not an induced subgraph in
O
. This completes the proof of
5.33
.
∎

Claim 5.34
.

u\rightarrow v
cannot be part of an induced subgraph
w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
in
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.b).

Proof of
5.34
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
in
O
.
Since
w\rightarrow v\in O
and
w\rightarrow u\notin O
, from
5.32
,
w\in\mathcal{C}
. This implies
u,v,w\in V_{M_{1}}
, as
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
. Since
u,v,w\in\mathcal{C}
, and from the construction,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{G_{1}}]})=\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})
, we have
u-v-w
is an induced subgraph in
M_{1}
. This further implies that
u-v-w
is an induced subgraph in
O_{1}
as
V_{O_{1}}=V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{O}
, and
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
. This contradicts
item

2
of
definition

3.8
. This implies that our assumption is wrong. This completes the proof of
5.34
.
∎

Claim 5.35
.

u\rightarrow v
cannot be part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
in
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.c).

Proof of
5.35
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
in
O
.
Since
w\rightarrow v\in O
and
w\rightarrow u\notin O
, from
5.32
,
w\in\mathcal{C}
. This implies
u,v,w\in V_{O}\cap V_{\mathcal{C}}
. From the choice of
u
and
v
that we have (
items

1
and
2
) there cannot exist any
w
such that
u\rightarrow w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u\in O
. This implies that our assumption is wrong. This completes the proof of
5.35
.
∎

Claim 5.36
.

u\rightarrow v
cannot be part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w-u-w^{\prime}\rightarrow v
in
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.d).

Proof of
5.36
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w-u-w^{\prime}\rightarrow v
in
O
.
Since
w\rightarrow v,w^{\prime}\rightarrow v\in O
and
w\rightarrow u,w^{\prime}\rightarrow u\notin O
, from
5.32
,
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. This implies
u,v,w,w^{\prime}\in V_{M_{1}}
, as
\mathcal{C}
is an undirected connected component of
M_{1}
. Since
w,v,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, and from the construction,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{G_{1}}]})=\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})
, we have
w-v-w^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph in
M_{1}
. This further implies that
w-v-w^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph in
O_{1}
as
V_{O_{1}}=V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{O}
, and
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
. This contradicts
item

2
of
definition

3.8
. This implies that our assumption is wrong. This completes the proof of
5.36
.
∎

5.33
,
5.34
,
5.35
and
5.36
imply that
u\rightarrow v
is not strongly protected in
O
, contradicting
item

3
of
definition

3.8
. This implies our assumption that there exists a directed edge in
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
is wrong. This further implies
O[V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}]
is an undirected graph. This completes the proof of
5.28
.
∎

Proof of
5.24
.

Contrary to
5.24
, suppose for some
i\in\{1,2\}
, there exist nodes
u
,
v
, and
w
belonging to an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{i}
such that
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{i}\neq\varnothing
, and
(u,v,w,u)
is a directed cycle in
M
. W.l.o.g., let us assume
i=1
.

Claim 5.37
.

No node of the cycle belongs to
r_{1}
.

Proof.

If a node of the cycle belongs to
r_{1}
then all the nodes of the cycle are in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})\subseteq V_{O}
. From
3.13
,
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
O
contains the directed cycle. But, this is a contradiction as
O
is a partial MEC (
definition

2.3
), and a partial MEC is a chain graph. This implies no node of the cycle belongs to
r_{1}
.
∎

Claim 5.38
.

At least one node of the cycle is not in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.

Proof.

Contrary to
5.38
, suppose all the nodes of the cycle belong to
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. Since
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})\subseteq V_{O}
. From
3.13
,
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
O
contains the directed cycle. But, this is a contradiction as
O
is a partial MEC (
definition

2.3
), and a partial MEC is a chain graph. This implies our assumption is incorrect. This validates
5.38
.
∎

Since
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}\neq\varnothing
, while running step
2
of the construction of
M
, at some iteration,
\mathcal{C}
gets considered at step
2
of the construction of
M
. Let
\tau
be the LBFS ordering of
\mathcal{C}
constructed at step
2a
of the construction of
M
. Since
\tau
starts with
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}
(from step
2a
of the construction of
M
), the following claims are intuitive:

Claim 5.39
.

For two nodes
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, if
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}
and
y\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}
then
\tau(x)<\tau(y)
.

Claim 5.40
.

For two nodes
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, if
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap(r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1}))
and
y\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap(r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1}))
then
\tau(x)<\tau(y)
.

The following claim comes from the implementation of step
2b
of the construction of
M
.

Claim 5.41
.

For
u-v\in\mathcal{C}
, if
v\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, and
\tau(u)<\tau(v)
then
v\rightarrow u\notin M
.

Proof.

Contrary to
5.41
, suppose
v\rightarrow u\in M
.
From
5.26
, either
v\rightarrow u
has been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.

If
v\rightarrow u
is added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
then
v\rightarrow u\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. From
5.27
,
v\rightarrow u\in O
. This implies
v,u\in V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{O}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. But, this is a contradiction, as we have given
v\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. This implies
v\rightarrow u
is not added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
.

Since
\tau(u)<\tau(v)
, the precondition of step
2b
implies
v\rightarrow u
cannot be added to
M
at step
2
.

The above discussion implies
v\rightarrow u\notin M
. This completes the proof of
5.41
.
∎

W.l.o.g., let us assume that
w
has the highest rank in
\tau
among the nodes in the directed cycle
(u,v,w,u)
, i.e.,
\tau(u)<\tau(w)
and
\tau(v)<\tau(w)
.

We first show that all the edges of the cycle
(u,v,w,u)
are not directed in
M
. All the edges of the cycle
(u,v,w,u)
are directed in
M
implies
u\rightarrow v,v\rightarrow w,w\rightarrow u\in M
.
Since
\tau(u)<\tau(w)
, from
5.41
,
w\rightarrow u\notin M
. This implies all three edges of the cycle are not directed in
M
.

We now show that no two edges of the cycle
(u,v,w,u)
are directed.
Since
w\rightarrow v\notin M
(discussed in the previous paragraph), if two edges are directed then it must be
u\rightarrow v
and
v\rightarrow w
.
From
5.26
, the two directed edges must have been added to
M
either at step
1
or at step
2
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.
Since
w
is of highest rank in
\tau
among the nodes in the directed cycle, from

5.38
and
5.40
,
w\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})}
.
This implies that while running step
2b
,
u\rightarrow w
gets added to
M
due to the preconditions of step
2(b)ii
get satisfied. This implies
(u,v,w,u)
is not a cycle in
M
, a contradiction. Therefore, no two edges of the cycle
(u,v,w,u)
are directed.

We now show that not even a single edge of cycle
(u,v,w,u)
of
M
is directed. Since
w\rightarrow v\notin M
, if an edge of the cycle is directed then there are two possibilities: either
u\rightarrow v\in M
(that means
u\rightarrow v-w-u
is a directed cycle in
M
) or
v\rightarrow w\in M
(that means
u-v\rightarrow w-u
is a directed cycle in
M
). The following claims show that both possibilities do not occur.

Claim 5.42
.

There do not exist nodes
u,v,w\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u\rightarrow v-w-u\in M
,
w\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, and rank of
w
in
\tau
is highest among the nodes in the cycle, where
\tau
is the LBFS ordering of
\mathcal{C}
that we get at step
2a
of the construction of
M
.

Claim 5.43
.

There do not exist nodes
u,v,w\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u-v\rightarrow w-u\in M
, and rank of
w
in
\tau
is highest among the nodes in the cycle, where
\tau
is the LBFS ordering of
\mathcal{C}
that we get at step
2a
of the construction of
M
.

5.42
and
5.43
imply that none of the edges of the cycle
(u,v,w,u)
in
M
is directed. This achieves our goal of ensuring that
(u,v,u,v)
does not form a directed cycle in
M
. This completes the proof of
5.24
.
∎

Proof of
5.42
.

Suppose there exist nodes
u,v,w\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u\rightarrow v-w-u\in M
,
w\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, and rank of
w
in
\tau
is highest among the nodes in the cycle, where
\tau
is the LBFS ordering of
\mathcal{C}
that we get at step
2a
of the construction of
M
. Let
Y=\{y:y\in V_{\mathcal{C}},\tau(y)<\tau(w)
, and
u\rightarrow y-w-u\in M\}
. We pick a
v\in Y
such that for all
y\in Y
,
y\rightarrow v\notin M
.
We first show that such
v
always exists.

Suppose there does not exist a
v\in Y
such that for all
y\in Y
,
y\rightarrow v\notin M
. Then, there exists a cycle
C_{1}=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m},v_{m+1}=v_{1})
in
M
such that for
1\leq i\leq m
,
v_{i}\in Y
, and
v_{i}\rightarrow v_{i+1}\in M
. Since nodes in
Y
are in
V_{\mathcal{C}}
, we pick the node
v_{j}
among the nodes in the cycle
C_{1}
which has the highest rank in
\tau
.
This implies
\tau(v_{j})>\tau(v_{j+1})
. But, then, from
5.41
,
v_{j}\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, otherwise, we do not have
v_{j}\rightarrow v_{j+1}\in M
. Since
v_{j}
is the highest rank node among the nodes in
C_{1}
, from
5.40
, each node of the cycle is in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. This implies for
1\leq i\leq m
,
u_{i}-u_{i+1}\in\mathcal{C}
does not get directed at step
2
of the construction of
M
. Then, from
5.26
and
5.27
, for
1\leq i\leq m
,
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{i+1}\in O
. This implies
O
has a directed cycle, a contradiction, as
O
is a partial MEC (
definition

2.3
). This implies our assumption is not correct, and
v
always exists.

The following claim provides us the potential induced subgraph of
M
that contains
u\rightarrow v
.

Claim 5.44
.

For some
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
,
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
of
M
, or
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
of
M
.

5.44
provides two possible induced subgraphs of
M
that can contain
u\rightarrow v
. We go through both of them.

1.

Suppose there exists
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
of
M
. We first claim that
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
.

Suppose
\tau(w)<\tau(x)
. Since
w\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{1}\cap N(r_{1},G_{1})}
, from
5.40
,
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{1}\cap N(r_{1},G_{1})}
. Also, since
\tau(u)<\tau(w)
, we have
\tau(u)<\tau(x)
. But, then, from
5.41
,
x\rightarrow u\notin M
, a contradiction, as we have assumed
x\rightarrow u\rightarrow v\in M
. This implies
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
.

We now claim that
x-w\notin\mathcal{C}
. Suppose
x-w\in\mathcal{C}
. As
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
,
\tau(v)<\tau(w)
, and
x-w,v-w\in\mathcal{C}
, from
definition

2.10
,
x-v\in\mathcal{C}
. From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{\mathcal{C}}]})=\mathcal{C}
. This implies there is an edge between
x
and
v
in
M
. This further implies
x\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
is not an induced subgraph of
M
, contradicting our assumption. Therefore,
x-w\notin\mathcal{C}
.

As discussed above,
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
,
\tau(u)<\tau(w)
, and
x\rightarrow u\in M
. This satisfies the precondition of step
2(b)i
of the construction of
M
to make
u\rightarrow w\in M
. But, from our assumption
u-w\in M
, a contradiction. This implies this possibility cannot occur.

2.

Suppose there exists
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
of
M
. We first show that
x\in Y
(Recall
Y=\{y:y\in V_{\mathcal{C}},\tau(y)<\tau(w)
, and
u\rightarrow y-w-u\in M\}
). To prove that
x\in Y
, we have to show that
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, and
x-w\in M
.

Suppose
\tau(w)<\tau(x)
. Since
\tau(v)<\tau(w)
, we have
\tau(v)<\tau(x)
. Also, since
w\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap(r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1}))
, and
\tau(w)<\tau(x)
, from
5.40
,
x\notin V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap(r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1}))
. But, then, from
5.41
,
x\rightarrow v\notin M
, contradicting our assumption that
u\rightarrow x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u\in M
. This implies
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
.

Suppose
x-w\notin\textup{skeleton}({M})
. Then,
x\rightarrow v-w
is an induced subgraph of
M
such that
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, and
\tau(v)<\tau(w)
. Then, from step
2(b)i
of the construction of
M
, we have
v\rightarrow w\in M
, a contradiction, as we have assumed
u\rightarrow v-w-u
is a directed cycle in
M
. This implies
x-w\in\textup{skeleton}({M})
. This implies either
x\rightarrow w\in M
or
w\rightarrow x\in M
or
x-w\in M
. We show that the first two possibility does not occur. If
x\rightarrow w\in M
then we have a directed cycle
(u,x,w,u)
with two directed edges. But, we have shown earlier that there cannot exist a directed cycle of length two with two directed edges. This implies
x\rightarrow w\notin M
. Since
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, and
w\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, from
5.41
,
w\rightarrow x\notin M
. Thus, the only option that remains is
x-w\in M
.

The above discussion implies that
x\in Y
. But, from the choice of
v
, there cannot exist any
x\in Y
such that
x\rightarrow v\in M
, a contradiction. This implies our assumption is wrong, and there does not any
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
in
M
.

The above discussion implies that
u\rightarrow v
does not obey
5.44
, a contradiction.
This implies our assumption is wrong, and there do not exist nodes
u,v,w\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u\rightarrow v-w-u\in M
. This completes the proof of
5.42
.
∎

Proof of
5.43
.

Suppose there exist
u,v,w\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u-v\rightarrow w-u\in M
, and rank of
w
in
\tau
is highest among the nodes in the cycle, where
\tau
is the LBFS ordering of
\mathcal{C}
that we get at step
2a
of the construction of
M
. We pick
u,v,w
with least
\tau(w)
, i.e., for any other triplet
u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u^{\prime}-v^{\prime}\rightarrow w^{\prime}-u^{\prime}\in M
, and rank of
w^{\prime}
in
\tau
is highest among the nodes in the cycle
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime},u^{\prime})
then
\tau(w)<\tau(w^{\prime})
.

The following claim provides the potential induced subgraphs of
M
that contain
v\rightarrow w
.

Claim 5.45
.

For some
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
,
v\rightarrow w
is part of an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow v\rightarrow w
or
v\rightarrow x\rightarrow w\leftarrow v
in
M
.

5.45
provides two possible induced subgraphs of
M
that contains
v\rightarrow w
. We go through both of them.

1.

Suppose there exists
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, and
v\rightarrow w
is part of an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow v\rightarrow w
of
M
. We claim that
x-u\in\mathcal{C}
.

Suppose
x-u\notin\mathcal{C}
. From
5.26
,
x\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
either at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.

Suppose
x\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
.
This implies
x\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. From
5.27
,
x\rightarrow v\in O
. Now, if
u\in V_{O}
then
x\rightarrow v-u
is an induced subgraph in
O
, contradicting
O
as a partial MEC (
definition

2.3
). And, if
u\notin V_{O}
then at step
2(b)i
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered, we get
v\rightarrow u\in M
, contradicting
(u,v,w,u)
is a cycle. Thus, in each case of this possibility, we get a contradiction. We move to the second possibility.

Suppose
x\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered. Then, from the preconditions of steps
2b
of the construction of
M
, we have
\tau(x)<\tau(v)
. Then,
\tau(v)<\tau(u)
, otherwise, if
\tau(u)<\tau(v)
then from
definition

2.10
,
x-u\in\mathcal{C}
, contradicting our assumption. But, if
\tau(v)<\tau(u)
, then at step
2(b)i
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered, we get
v\rightarrow u\in M
, contradicting
(u,v,w,u)
is a cycle.

Thus, in each case of both possibilities, we get a contradiction. This implies
x-u\in\mathcal{C}
.

From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{\mathcal{C}}]})=\mathcal{C}
. Therefore, we have three possibilities: either
u\rightarrow x\in M
or
x\rightarrow u\in M
or
x-u\in M
. We show that none of the possibilities occurs.

If
u\rightarrow x\in M
then we get a directed cycle
(u,x,v,u)
in
M
with two directed edges. We have shown earlier that
M
does not have a directed cycle of length three with two directed edges. This implies
u\rightarrow x\notin M
.

Suppose
x\rightarrow u\in M
. Since
x\rightarrow v\rightarrow w
is an induced subgraph in
M
,
x-w\notin\textup{skeleton}({M})
. This implies
x\rightarrow u-w
is an induced subgraph in
M
with
w\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)
,
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, and
\tau(u)<\tau(w)
. This satisfies the precondition of step
2(b)i
of the construction of
M
for adding
u\rightarrow w
. And, therefore, while running step
2
of the construction of
M
for the undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
, we add
u\rightarrow w
at step
2(b)i
. But, this creates a contradiction, as from our assumption
u-w\in M
(as
u-v\rightarrow w-u
is a cycle in
M
).

If
x-u\in M
then we get a directed cycle
(x,v,u,x)
in
M
such that the rank of all the three nodes of the cycle in
\tau
is less than
\tau(w)
. From
5.38
, one node of the cycle does not belong to
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. From
5.40
, among the nodes of the cycle, the node with the highest rank does not belong to
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)
. From
5.41
,
x
cannot be the highest rank node. This implies either
u
or
v
is the highest rank node. If
u
is the highest rank node then
M
contains a cycle
x\rightarrow v-u-x
such that the rank of all the three nodes of the cycle in
\tau
is less than
\tau(u)
.
5.42
implies that such a cycle does not exist.
And, if
v
is the highest rank node then we get a directed cycle
u-x\rightarrow v-x
in
M
with the highest rank node is
\tau(v)
. Since
\tau(v)<\tau(w)
, this contradicts our choice of
w
. This implies this case also cannot occur.

The above discussion implies that in each case of this possibility, we get a contradiction. This implies our assumption is wrong. We now move to the second possibility of
5.45
.

2.

Suppose there exists
x\in V_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, and
v\rightarrow w
is part of an induced subgraph
v\rightarrow x\rightarrow w\leftarrow v
of
M
.

Given
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
,
\tau(u)<\tau(w)
, and
x-w,u-w\in\mathcal{C}
, from
definition

2.10
,
x-u\in\mathcal{C}
.
From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{\mathcal{C}}]})=\mathcal{C}
. Therefore, we have three possibilities: either
u\rightarrow x\in M
or
x\rightarrow u\in M
or
u-x\in M
. We show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose
u\rightarrow x\in M
. Then, while running step
2
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered, we add
u\rightarrow w
in
M
as all the preconditions of step
item

2(b)ii
is obeyed. But, this contradicts our assumption that
(u,v,w,u)
is a cycle in
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow x\notin M
.

Suppose
x\rightarrow u\in M
. Then,
M
has a directed cycle
(v,x,u,v)
with two directed edges. As discussed earlier, there cannot exist a directed cycle of length three with two directed edges. This implies
x\rightarrow u\notin M
.

Suppose
u-x\in M
. Then, we get a directed cycle
(u,v,x,u)
in
M
such that the rank of all the three nodes of the cycle in
\tau
is less than
\tau(w)
.
From
5.38
, one node of the cycle does not belong to
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.
From
5.40
, among the nodes of the cycle, the node with the highest rank does not belong to
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)
.
From
5.41
,
u
cannot be the highest rank node.
This implies either
u
or
x
is the highest rank node.
If
u
is the highest rank node then
M
contains a cycle
v\rightarrow x-u-v
such that
u
has the highest rank in
\tau
among nodes in the cycle.
5.42
implies that such a cycle does not exist. And, if
x
is the highest rank node then we get a directed cycle
u-v\rightarrow x-u
in
M
with the highest rank node being
x
. Since
\tau(x)<\tau(w)
, this contradicts our choice of
w
. This implies this cannot occur.

The above discussion implies
v\rightarrow w
is part of none of the induced subgraphs mentioned in
5.45
. This contradicts
5.45
. This implies that our assumption is wrong. This validates
5.43
.
∎

Proof of
5.44
.

From
5.26
,
u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
either at
step
1
of the construction of
M
, or it has been added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
, when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.

If
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
then
u\rightarrow v
obeys
5.44
(see step
2
of the construction of
M
).

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. From
5.27
,
u\rightarrow v\in O
. This implies
u,v\in V_{M_{1}}\cap V_{O}=V_{O_{1}}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. From
5.37
,
u,v\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
. Since
u,v\in N(r_{1},G_{1})\setminus r_{1}
, and
I=r_{1}\cap r_{2}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
, neighbors of
u
and
v
belong to
V_{G_{1}}
.
Since
u,v\in V_{O_{1}}
, and
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
,
u-v\in O_{1}
. From
item

3
of
definition

3.8
,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
. This implies
u\rightarrow v
is part of one of the induced subgraphs of
O
as shown in
fig.

1
.

1.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
because it is part of an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
of
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.a). From
3.13
,
x\rightarrow u\in M
.
As discussed earlier, the neighbors of
u
and
v
are in
V_{G_{1}}
. This implies
x\in V_{G_{1}}
.
From
corollary

5.19
, either
x-u\in M_{1}
or
x\rightarrow u\in M
.
From
item

3
of
theorem

2.2
,
x\rightarrow u-v
cannot be an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
x-u\in M_{1}
. This implies
x\in\mathcal{C}
. This implies
u\rightarrow v
obeys
5.44
.

2.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
because it is part of an induced subgraph
x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
of
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.b). From
3.13
,
x\rightarrow v\in M
.
As discussed earlier, the neighbors of
u
and
v
are in
V_{G_{1}}
. This implies
x\in V_{G_{1}}
.
From
corollary

5.19
, either
x-v\in M_{1}
or
x\rightarrow v\in M
.
From
item

3
of
theorem

2.2
,
x\rightarrow u-v
cannot be an induced subgraph of
M
. Therefore,
x-u\in M_{1}
. Since
u,v,x\in V_{O}\cap V_{M_{1}}=V_{O_{1}}
, and
\textup{skeleton}({O_{1}})=\textup{skeleton}({O[V_{O_{1}}]})
,
x-v-u
is an induced subgraph of
O_{1}
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(O_{1})\neq\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{1}}])
(as
x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u\in O
and
x-v-u\in O_{1}
). This contradicts
item

2
of
definition

3.8
. This implies this case cannot occur.

3.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
because it is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
of
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.c). From
3.13
,
u\rightarrow x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u\in M
.
As discussed earlier, the neighbors of
u
and
v
are in
V_{G_{1}}
. This implies
x\in V_{G_{1}}
.
From
corollary

5.19
, (a) either
u-x\in M_{1}
or
u\rightarrow x\in M
, and (b) either
x-v\in M_{1}
or
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
. If either
u\rightarrow x\in M_{1}
or
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
then
(u,x,v,u)
is a directed cycle in
M_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
. This implies
u-x,x-v\in M_{1}
, and
x\in\mathcal{C}
. This further implies
u\rightarrow v
obeys
5.44
.

4.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
because it is part of an induced subgraph
u-x\rightarrow v\leftarrow u-x^{\prime}\rightarrow v
of
O
(as shown in
fig.

1
.d). From
3.13
,
x\rightarrow v\in M
.
As discussed earlier, the neighbors of
u
and
v
are in
V_{G_{1}}
. This implies
x\in V_{G_{1}}
.
From
corollary

5.19
, either
x-v\in M_{1}
or
x\rightarrow v\in M
. From
corollary

5.20
,
u-x\in M_{1}
. If
x\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
then
(u,x,v,u)
is a directed cycle in
M_{1}
, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
. This implies
x-v\in M_{1}
. Similarly,
x^{\prime}-v\in M_{1}
. Since
u,v,x,x^{\prime}\in V_{O}\cap V_{M_{1}}=V_{O_{1}}
, and
\textup{skeleton}({O_{1}})=\textup{skeleton}({O[V_{O_{1}}]})
,
x-v-x^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph of
O_{1}
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(O_{1})\neq\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{1}}])
(as
x\rightarrow v\leftarrow x^{\prime}\in O
and
x-v-x^{\prime}\in O_{1}
). This contradicts
item

2
of
definition

3.8
. This implies this case cannot occur.

The above discussion implies that
u\rightarrow v
obeys
5.44
. This completes the proof of
5.44
.
∎

Proof of
5.45
.

From
5.26
, either
v\rightarrow w
has been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or it has been added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.

Suppose
v\rightarrow w
has been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
. This implies
v\rightarrow w\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. From
5.27
,
v\rightarrow w\in O
. This implies
w\in V_{O}\cap V_{M_{1}}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
, a contradiction, as we have assumed
w\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. This implies
v\rightarrow w
has not been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
.

If
v\rightarrow w
is added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
then
v\rightarrow w
obeys
5.45
(see step
2
of the construction of
M
).

This implies
v\rightarrow w
obeys
5.45
. This completes the proof of
5.45
.
∎

Proof of
3.16
.

Suppose there exists an undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M
such that
\mathcal{C}
is not chordal. Then, there must exist a chordless cycle
C=(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{l},u_{l+1}=u_{0})
in
\mathcal{C}
. Since
G
is a chordal graph,
C
cannot be a chordless cycle in
G
. This implies there must exist an edge
u_{i}-u_{j}
between two non-adjacent nodes of
C
in
G
. W.l.o.g., we assume
i<j
.
Since the skeleton of
M
is
G
, either
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{j}\in M
, or
u_{j}\rightarrow u_{i}\in M
, or
u_{i}-u_{j}\in M
.

If
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{j}\in M
then
(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{i},u_{j},u_{j+1},\ldots,u_{l},u_{l+1}=u_{0})
is a directed cycle in
M
. If
u_{j}\rightarrow u_{i}\in M
then
(u_{j},u_{i},u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{j})
is a directed cycle in
M
. Both of them contradict
3.15
, which asserts that
M
is a chain graph.

Thus, the only option that remains is
u_{i}-u_{j}\in M
. But, then,
C
is not a chordless cycle in
\mathcal{C}
, contradicting our assumption that
\mathcal{C}
is not chordal. This completes the proof of
3.16
.
∎

Proof of
3.17
.

Suppose
M
has an induced subgraph
a\rightarrow b-c
.
Since
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
, either
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
a,b,c\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
a\in V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
,
b\in I
and
c\in V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
.
We one by one show that none of the possibilities occurs.

Suppose
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph of
M
such that
a,b,c\in V_{G_{1}}
.
Since the skeleton of
M_{1}
and the skeleton of
M[V_{G_{1}}]
both are the same, from
corollaries

5.19
and
5.20
, either
a\rightarrow b-c
or
a-b-c
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC, from
item

3
of
theorem

2.2
,
a\rightarrow b-c
is cannot be an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
. Thus, the only option that remains is
a-b-c
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
. This implies
a,b
and
c
belong to the same undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{1}
. From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{\mathcal{C}}]})=\mathcal{C}
. This implies
a-b,b-c\in\mathcal{C}
(there cannot exist a directed edge between two nodes of the same undirected connected component of an MEC, otherwise, we get a directed cycle, contradicting
item

1
of
theorem

2.2
). Since
a-b\in\mathcal{C}
, and
a\rightarrow b\in M
, from
5.25
,
V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap r_{1}\neq\varnothing
. From
5.26
,
a\rightarrow b
is added to
M
either at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or at step
2
of the construction of
M
when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered.

Suppose
a\rightarrow b
is added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
. This implies
a\rightarrow b\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. Then, from
5.27
,
a\rightarrow b\in O
. This implies
a,b\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. This further implies
c\notin r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. Otherwise,
a\rightarrow b-c
is an induced subgraph of
O
, contradicting
definition

2.3
(recall that
O
is a partial MEC). This implies
a,b\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap V_{O}=V_{\mathcal{C}}\cap(r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1}))
, and
c\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})}
. From
5.40
,
\tau(a)<\tau(c)
, and
\tau(b)<\tau(c)
. Then, at step
2
of the construction of
M
, when
\mathcal{C}
has been considered, while running step
2(b)i
, we get
b\rightarrow c\in M
, a contradiction, since
b-c\in M
.

Suppose
a\rightarrow b
is added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
.
Let
\tau
be the LBFS ordering of
\mathcal{C}
returned at step
2a
of the construction of
M
. Then,
\tau(a)<\tau(b)
. There cannot be
\tau(c)<\tau(b)
, otherwise, from
definition

2.10
, we have
a-c\in\mathcal{C}
, which implies
a-b-c
is not an induced subgraph of
M
, a contradiction. This further implies
\tau(b)<\tau(c)
. Then, while running step
2(b)i
, we get
b\rightarrow c\in M
, a contradiction, since
b-c\in M
. In every case, we get a contradiction. This implies that the possibility that
a,b,c,\in V_{G_{1}}
cannot occur.

Similarly, we can claim that
a,b,c\notin V_{G_{2}}
. And, if
a=V_{G_{1}}\setminus{I}
,
b\in I
, and
c\in V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
then
a,b,c\in V_{O}
. From
3.13
,
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
a\rightarrow b-c\in O
. But, this is a contradiction, as
O
is a partial MEC (
definition

2.3
). This implies this case also cannot occur. This further implies that there cannot exist an induced subgraph
a\rightarrow b-c
in
M
. This completes the proof of
3.17
.
∎

Proof of
3.18
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is a directed edge in
M
. From the construction of
M
, there are two possibilities: either
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
, or
u,v\in V_{G_{2}}
. W.l.o.g., let us assume
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
.

Suppose
u,v\in V_{G_{1}}
and
u\rightarrow v\in M
. From
corollary

5.19
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
or
u-v\in M_{1}
. We show that in both cases
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M
.

1.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
. According to
item

4
of
theorem

2.2
,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M_{1}
. From
5.18
, a directed edge in
M_{1}
is a directed edge in
M
. Now, let’s consider the induced subgraphs shown in (a), (b), (c), and (d) of
fig.

1
.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M_{1}
due to its inclusion in an induced subgraph shown in (a), (b), or (c) of
fig.

1
. From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})=\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{M_{1}}]})
. And, from
5.18
, a directed edge in
M_{1}
is a directed edge in
M
. This implies that the induced subgraph is also an induced subgraph of
M
. This further implies
u\rightarrow v
is also strongly protected in
M
.

Now, let’s focus on the case where
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M_{1}
because it is part of the induced subgraph shown in
fig.

1
-(d). In this induced subgraph, the edges
w\rightarrow v
,
w^{\prime}\rightarrow v
, and
u\rightarrow v
are directed, indicating that
w\rightarrow v
,
w^{\prime}\rightarrow v
, and
u\rightarrow v
are also present in
M
(from
5.18
).
To establish the strong protection of
u\rightarrow v
in
M
, we consider different cases. If
w-u
and
w^{\prime}-u
are present in
M
, then
u\rightarrow v
is part of the induced subgraph shown in
fig.

1
-(d), making it strongly protected in
M
. If
u\rightarrow w
or
u\rightarrow w^{\prime}
exists in
M
, then again
u\rightarrow v
is part of an induced subgraph, either as shown in
fig.

1
-(c) with
u\rightarrow w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
, or as shown in
fig.

1
-(c) with
u\rightarrow w^{\prime}\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
. In both cases,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M
.

The remaining possibility is that both
w\rightarrow u
and
w^{\prime}\rightarrow u
are present in
M
. We show that this case cannot occur.
Since
w-u-w^{\prime}
is present in
M_{1}
, it implies that
w
,
u
, and
w^{\prime}
belong to the same undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{1}
.

Suppose
w,u,w^{\prime}\in r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})=V_{O_{1}}
. Since
O_{1}=M_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})]
,
w-u-w^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph in
O_{1}
. Also,
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})\subseteq V_{O}
, and from
3.13
,
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
w\rightarrow u\leftarrow w^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph of
O
. But, this is a contradiction, as since
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
, from
item

2
,
\mathcal{V}(O_{1})=\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{1}}])
. This implies
w,u,w^{\prime}
all are not in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
.

Suppose all the nodes
w,u,w^{\prime}
are not in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. If for an edge
x-y
of
M_{1}
,
x\rightarrow y
is added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
then
x\rightarrow y\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. From
5.27
,
x\rightarrow y\in O
. This implies
x,y\in V_{O}\cap V_{M_{1}}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. As per our assumption, not all the nodes
w,u,w^{\prime}
are in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
. This implies at least one edge among
w\rightarrow u
and
w^{\prime}\rightarrow u
added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
.
W.l.o.g., let us assume
w\rightarrow u
is added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
. Then, from the precondition of
item

2b
,
\tau(w)<\tau(u)
, and
u\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})}
. And, since
w^{\prime}\rightarrow u\in M
, from
5.41
,
\tau(w^{\prime})<\tau(u)
. But, then, from
definition

2.10
,
w-w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, a contradiction, as
w-u-w^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph in
M_{1}
. This implies this case cannot occur.

In conclusion, we have shown that in all possible cases, if
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M_{1}
, then it is also strongly protected in
M
. Hence, the claim is verified.

2.

Suppose
u-v\in M_{1}
. This implies
u
and
v
belong to the same undirected connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
M_{1}
.

u\rightarrow v
has been added to
M
either at step
1
of the construction of
M
, or at step
2
of the construction of
M
.

If
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
then
u\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. From
5.27
,
u\rightarrow v\in O
. This implies
u,v\in V_{O}\cap V_{M_{1}}=V_{O_{1}}
. Since
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
, this implies
u-v\in O_{1}
and
u\rightarrow v\in O
. Then, from
item

3
of
definition

3.8
,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
O
. From
3.13
,
O
is an induced subgraph of
M
. This implies
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M
as well.

Suppose
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
.
Then either it is part of an induced subgraph
w\rightarrow u\rightarrow v
(from step
2(b)i
), as shown in
fig.

1
.a, or it is part of an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow w\rightarrow v\leftarrow u
in
M
(from step
2(b)ii
), as shown in
fig.

1
.c. In both cases,
u\rightarrow v
is strongly protected in
M
.

We show that in all the possibilities, if
u\rightarrow v\in M
then it is strongly protected in
M
. This completes the proof of
3.18
.
∎

Proof of
3.19
.

We first prove that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}})=M_{1}
.
Since
M_{1}
is an MEC of
G_{1}
, from
definition

2.5
, to prove
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}})=M_{1}
, we only need to show
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})=\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])
.

We first show that
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])
. From step
1
of the construction of
M
, all directed edges of
M_{1}
are also directed edges of
M
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])
, as from the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M_{1}})=\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{G_{1}}]})
.

We now show that
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
. Suppose
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])\nsubseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
. Then, there must exist
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
such that
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M[V_{G_{1}}]
, and
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\notin M_{1}
. From
corollary

5.19
, either
u\rightarrow v\in M_{1}
or
u-v\in M_{1}
. Similarly, either
v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
, or
v-w\in M_{1}
. Since
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w\notin M_{1}
(from our assumption), either
u\rightarrow v-w\in M_{1}
, or
u-v\leftarrow w\in M_{1}
, or
u-v-w\in M_{1}
. Since
M_{1}
is an MEC, from
item

3
of
theorem

2.2
, the first two possibilities are not possible. Thus, the only option that remains is
u-v-w\in M_{1}
. This implies
u,v,w
belongs to the same undirected connected component (say
\mathcal{C}
) of
M_{1}
.

u\rightarrow v
and
w\rightarrow v
have not been added to
M
at step
1
of the construction of
M
. Otherwise,
u\rightarrow v,w\rightarrow v\in U_{M}(M_{1},M_{2},O)
. But, then, from
5.27
,
u\rightarrow v,w\rightarrow v\in O
. This further implies
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{O}=V_{O_{1}}
. Since
O_{1}
is an induced subgraph of
M_{1}
, this implies
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
O[V_{O_{1}}]
and not in
O_{1}
. This contradicts
item

2
of
definition

3.8
, as
O
is an extension of
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
. This implies that out of
u\rightarrow v
and
w\rightarrow v
, at least one has been added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
.

W.l.o.g., let us assume
u\rightarrow v
is added to
M
at step
2
of the construction of
M
. Then, from the precondition of
item

2b
,
\tau(u)<\tau(v)
, and
v\in V_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})}
. And, since
w\rightarrow v\in M
, from
5.41
,
\tau(w)<\tau(v)
. But, then, from
definition

2.10
,
u-w\in\mathcal{C}
. From the construction of
M
,
\textup{skeleton}({M[V_{\mathcal{C}}]})=\mathcal{C}
. This further implies there cannot be an induced subgraph
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
in
M
, contradicting our assumption. This implies our assumption is wrong. This shows
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
. This further implies
\mathcal{V}(M[V_{G_{1}}])=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})
.

Similarly, we can show that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{2}})=M_{2}
. This completes the proof of
3.19
.
∎

Proof of
3.20
.

Since
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
and
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
,
\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\mathcal{V}(O)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M)
.
Suppose
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M
.
Since
I=r_{1}\cap r_{2}
is a vertex separator of
G
, either
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
or
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
, or
u,v,w\in V_{O}
. Since
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
, if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{1}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{1}
, if
u,v,w\in V_{G_{2}}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
M_{2}
, and if
u,v,w\in V_{O}
then
u\rightarrow v\leftarrow w
is a v-structure in
O
. This implies
\mathcal{V}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\mathcal{V}(O)
. This further implies
\mathcal{V}(M)=\mathcal{V}(M_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(M_{2})\cup\mathcal{V}(O)
. As discussed in the introduction, given a skeleton and a set of v-structures, there exists a unique MEC with that skeleton and v-structures. This implies that
M
is unique.
∎

Proof of
lemma

3.21
.

We use
definition

3.8
to define a bijective function
f_{O}
. We use the function for the computation of
|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
.

Definition 5.46

(Function
f_{O}
)
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
is a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
. Let
X_{1}=r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})
,
X_{2}=r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})
, and
X=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)
.
Consider
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])
. Define
T_{1}=\text{MEC}(G,O)
and
T_{2}=\{(M_{1},M_{2}):

\exists(O_{1},O_{2})\in O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])\times\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])
such that
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})
, and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})\}
. The function
f_{O}:T_{1}\rightarrow T_{2}
is defined as
f_{O}(M)=(M_{1},M_{2})
if
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Lemma 5.47
.

f_{O}
is bijective.

Proof.

We first prove
\rightarrow
of
lemma

5.47
.

Claim 5.48
.

For each
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O)
, there exists a unique
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
f_{O}(M)=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
M\in\text{MEC}(G,O))
. This implies
M
is an MEC of
G
, and
M[V_{O}]=O
. Let
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. From
2.6
, there exists a unique
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
\mathcal{P}(M,V_{G_{1}},V_{G_{2}})=(M_{1},M_{2})
. Thus, we only have to show that
(M_{1},M_{2})\in T_{2}
.
Let
O_{1}=M_{1}[X_{1}]
and
O_{2}=M_{2}[X_{2}]
. From
corollary

3.5
,
O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])
, and
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])
.
This implies
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
.
From
lemma

3.9
,
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
. From the definition of
T_{2}
(given at
definition

5.46
),
(M_{1},M_{2})\in T_{2}
. Thus, from
definition

5.46
,
f_{O}(M)=(M_{1},M_{2})
.

∎

We now prove
\leftarrow
of
lemma

5.47
.

Claim 5.49
.

Let
O_{1}
be a partial MEC of
G_{1}[X_{1}]
and

be a partial MEC of

such that

is an extension of
(O_{1},O_{2})
. For each

and

such that

.

Proof.

Lemmas

3.10
and
5.46

.
∎

5.48
and
5.49
prove

5.47
.
∎

From
lemma

5.47
,
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
. Since
T_{1}=\text{MEC}(G,O)
, therefore,
|T_{1}|=|\text{MEC}(G,O)|
. Since
T_{2}
contains tuples
(M_{1},M_{2})
such that for some tuple
(O_{1},O_{2})\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])\times\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])
, and
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
,
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})
and
M_{2}\in\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
. This implies

T_{2}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])\\
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])\\
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})\end{subarray}}{\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})}.

(18)

We show that sets
\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
are disjoint. Suppose sets
\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})

(M_{1},M_{2})
such that

as well as

.
This implies
M_{1}\in\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})
as well as

. But, from
corollary

3.4
, image of
M
on
X_{1}
is unique, i.e.,
O_{1}=O_{1}^{\prime}
. Similarly, we can say that
O_{2}=O_{2}^{\prime}
. This shows
(O_{1},O_{2})=(O_{1}^{\prime},O_{2}^{\prime})
. This implies that the sets
\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})\times\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})
are disjoint. This further implies

|T_{2}|=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[X_{1}])\\
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[X_{2}])\\
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})\end{subarray}}{|\text{MEC}(G_{1},O_{1})|\times|\text{MEC}(G_{2},O_{2})|}.

(19)

This proves
lemma

3.21
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

3.22
.

We start with the following:

\text{MEC}(G,O^{\prime})=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])\\
O^{\prime}=O[X^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}{\text{MEC}(G,O)}.

We first show that the L.H.S. of

20
is a subset of the R.H.S. of

20
.

Lemma 5.50
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph,
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\subseteq V_{G}
, and

.
Let

. Then, there exists a partial MEC

Since

, from

,

. Let
O=M[X]
. From

,

, and

. From

,

. Also, since

, and both

are induced subgraph of

,
O[X^{\prime}]=M[X^{\prime}]=O^{\prime}
. This proves

5.50
.
∎

We now show that the R.H.S. of

20

.

Lemma 5.51
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph,
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\subseteq V_{G}
,
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[X])
, and

.

.

Since

, and

. Also, since

, and

,

. This implies

is an image of

, i.e.,

. This proves

5.51
.
∎

lemmas

5.50
and
5.51
imply
eq.

20
.

From

3.4
, each MEC has a unique image on

. This implies the R.H.S. of

is a disjoint union of sets

for

. This further implies

. This completes the proof of

Algorithm 4

Is_Extension

Algorithm

is constructed to verify whether for any partial MECs

, and

Lemma 5.52
.

Let
G
be a chordal graph, and
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two induced subgraphs of
G
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and
I=V_{G_{1}}\cap V_{G_{2}}
be a vertex separator of
G
that separates
V_{G_{1}}\setminus I
and
V_{G_{2}}\setminus I
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be cliques of, respectively,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
r_{1}\cap r_{2}=I
.
O\in\text{PMEC}(G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)])
,
O_{1}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})])
, and
O_{2}\in\text{PMEC}(G_{2}[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})])
.
Algorithm

4
outputs 1 if
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
, otherwise, 0.

Proof.

Lines
4
-
4
verifies
item

1
of
definition

3.8
.
Since from the construction, for each
a\in\{1,2\}
,
\textup{skeleton}({O_{a}})=\textup{skeleton}({O[V_{O_{a}}]})
,
item

1
of
definition

3.8
implies that
\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{a}}])\subseteq\mathcal{V}(O_{a})
.
Lines
4
-
4
verifies
\mathcal{V}(O_{a})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(O[V_{O_{a}}])
. In this way,
item

2
of
definition

3.8
is verified.
Lines
4
-
4
verifies
item

3
of
definition

3.8
. If
items

1
,
2
and
3
of
definition

3.8
fails then the algorithm returns 0, otherwise, it returns 1. This validates
algorithm

4
.
∎

5.3
Omitted proofs of
section

4

Proof of
lemma

4.1
.

At line
1
,
Algorithm

1
cuts an edge of
G
. This creates two induced subgraphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
of
G
. At lines
1
-
1
, the algorithm recursively call itself for
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. Since the number of edges in
G
is
n-1
, the number of times
algorithm

1
recursively calls itself is
n-1
.
Computation of
N
,
b
, and
c_{0}
(at lines
1
-
1
) can be done in
O(d)
time.
The while loop at lines
1
-
1
runs for
O(d)
time. And, the computation of
c_{i}
at line
1
takes
O(d)
time. This implies the running time of lines
1
-
1
is
O(d^{2})
time. Thus, the overall running time of
algorithm

1
is
O(d^{2}n)
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

4.2
.

At line
2
of
algorithm

2
, we cut an edge of
T
to recursively call
algorithm

2
(at lines
2
and
2
). The number of edges in a clique tree of a chordal graph
G
is
O(n)
. This implies the number of times
algorithm

2
is called recursively is
O(n)
. If the treewidth of
G
is
k
then the size of
r_{1}
is
k+1
. If the degree of
G
is
d
then the number of nodes in
r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)
is at most
(d+1)(k+1)
, i.e.,
O(dk)
. And, the number of edges in
G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]
is
O(d^{2}k^{2})
. Since in a partial MEC of
G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]
, each edge
u-v
of
G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]
is either
u-v
or
u\rightarrow v
, or
v\rightarrow u
. This implies the number of partial MECs of
G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]
is at most
3^{d^{2}k^{2}}
, i.e.,
O(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})})
. Therefore, the running time of initialization of
f
(lines
2
-
2
) is
O(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})})
.
Running time of lines
2
-
2
takes
O(1)
time.
The running time of lines
2
–
2
is
O(|V_{G}|+|E_{G}|)
.
Similar to the partial MECs of
G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]
, the number of partial MECs of
G_{1}[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G_{1})]
,
G_{2}[r_{2}\cup N(r_{2},G_{2})]
, and
G[r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup N(r_{1}\cup r_{2},G)]
is
O(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})})
. Therefore, each of the for loops at lines
2
,
2
, and
2
runs for
O(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})})
time. Since the number of edges in
O,O_{1}
and
O_{2}
are
O(d^{2}k^{2})
, from
lemma

5.53
, we can verify whether
O\in\mathcal{E}(O_{1},O_{2})
(at line
2
) in
O(d^{2}k^{2})
time.
Construction of
O^{\prime}
at line
2
, can be done in
O(d^{2}k^{2})
time. And, updation of
f
at line
2
runs for
O(1)
time. This implies the overall run time of
algorithm

2
is
O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2}))
. This completes the proof of
lemma

4.2
.
∎

Proof of
lemma

4.3
.

We can compute a clique tree representation of a chordal graph
G
(line
3
of
algorithm

3
) in
O(|V_{G}|+|E_{G}|)
time
Blair and Peyton 1993
. From
lemma

4.2
, the running time of line
3
is
O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2}))
. As discussed in the proof of
lemma

4.2
, the number of partial MECs in
G[r_{1}\cup N(r_{1},G)]
is
O(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})})
. This implies the running time of lines
3
-
3
if
O(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})})
. This implies the overall running time of
algorithm

3
is
O(n(2^{O(d^{2}k^{2})}+n^{2}))
. This completes the proof of
lemma

4.3
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

4.4
.

We can pick a root node
r_{1}
of
G
in
O(1)
time. Then, we call
algorithm

1
for input
G
and
r_{1}
. From
lemma

4.1
, the running time of the algorithm is
O(d^{2}n)
. The algorithm returns
n_{1}(G,r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{0}(G,r_{1})
,
n_{0}^{1}(G,r_{1})
, …,
n_{0}^{d}(G,r_{1})
, where
d
is the degree of
r_{1}
in
G
. Then, using
eqs.

1
and
2
, we can compute
|\text{MEC}(G)|
. Therefore, in
O(d^{2}n)
time, we can count the MECs of
G
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

4.5
.

Lemmas

3.25
and
4.3
validates the theorem.
∎

Lemma 5.53
.

The time complexity of
algorithm

4
is
O(d^{3}k^{3})
.

Proof.

As discussed in the proof of
algorithm

2
, the number of nodes in
O,O_{1}
, and
O_{2}
is
O(dk)
, and the number of edges in them is
O(d^{2}k^{2})
.
This implies the running time of lines
4
-
4
is
O(d^{2}k^{2})
.
The running time of lines
4
-
4
is
O(d^{3}k^{3})
. Verifying each directed edge to determine whether it is strongly protected or not cumulatively takes
O(|V_{O}|+|E_{O}|)
time.
This implies the running time of lines
4
-
4
is
O(d^{2}k^{2})
. Therefore, the overall running time of
algorithm

4
is
O(d^{3}k^{3})
. This completes the proof of
lemma

5.53
.
∎
